## D

The Regional Government of Caribbean, along with the Ministries of Economy and Finance, the Ministries of Water Resources and Meteorology, and the National Environmental Sustainability Division (NESD), are the executing entity.

In 1985, the Ministry of Economy and Finance (MEF) was established to serve as the central government agency responsible for the economic and financial policies of the Republic of Caribbean. The ministry is responsible for MEF, assisted by its prime minister and deputy prime ministers. It is also responsible for the National Economic and Social Council (NESC), which provides advice on economic and social policy issues. The NESC is composed of representatives from various sectors, including the private sector, labor unions, and non-governmental organizations.

The Ministry of Water Resources and Meteorology (MOWRAM) will be the main executing entity for this project. MOWRAM is responsible for water resources management, meteorological services, and climate change adaptation. It is a key player in the implementation of the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) process and the development of the National Climate Change Policy.

The National Committee for Sub-National Democratic Development Secretariat (NCDDS), which was established to implement decentralization among provinces, will be responsible for developing sub-national climate change adaptation plans. The NCDDS is composed of representatives from various ministries, including the Ministry of Environment and Meteorology (MOEM) and the Ministry of Water Resources and Meteorology (MOWRAM). It is a key player in the implementation of the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) process.

### A.3.1 Executing Entity

The Ministry of Economy and Finance (MEF) will be the main executing entity for this project.

### A.21 Executive Summary

#### Climate change impacts

- Caribbean's agriculture sector is crucial for the economy but also highly susceptible to the effects of climate change.
- 128 million disaster events occurred between 1990 and 2016, with an average of one disaster event per year.
- The region has a high vulnerability index of 37%, and a vulnerability score of 54%.
- Climate change impacts include increased frequency and intensity of storms, droughts, and floods. The Caribbean is also highly vulnerable to sea-level rise.

#### Project objectives

The project aims to support the Government of Caribbean in implementing its National Adaptation Plan (NAP) process and developing a comprehensive climate change policy. It will also support the development of sub-national adaptation plans and provide technical assistance to provinces, districts, and communities.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V1.0 PAGE 1

- The objective of system modernisation and improvement of farmer resilience will be achieved by:
  - Enhancing agricultural productivity and doubling household incomes.
  - Increasing resilience to climate change through improved water management practices, capacity building, and climate-smart agriculture.
  - Greater market integration through value chain development and rural infrastructure.
  - Enhancing the livelihoods of smallholder farmers through improved access to finance and markets.
  - Strengthening the capacity of farmer organisations such as cooperatives on innovation in irrigation schemes and post-harvest handling (IPMUC) and Component 3: institutional strengthening of government institutions, particularly MOWRIAM.
  - Enhancing the capacity of local governments to implement climate change adaptation measures as the IPMUC. When the focus for MOWRIAM will be upgrading technical capacity in various aspects of agriculture.

## Climate results benefits

- The Climate Adaptive Irrigation and Sustainable Agriculture for Resilience (CAISAR) project will be implemented through a participatory approach that involves local communities, particularly women and youth.
  - The project will enhance the resilience of smallholder farmers to climate change by improving their access to water and agricultural advisory services, and high-quality input supplies.
  - The project will also improve the livelihoods of smallholder farmers by increasing their income through improved productivity and market integration.
  - The project will also contribute to the achievement of the national objective of reducing greenhouse gas emissions from agriculture by 30% by 2030.
  - The project will also promote the use of climate-smart technologies, such as solar-powered irrigation systems, which can help to reduce greenhouse gas emissions and improve water management.
  - The project will also contribute to the achievement of the national objective of increasing the productivity of smallholder farmers by 50% by 2030.

- The project is also expected to generate climate benefits as a result of enhancing resilience from adaptation by CAISAR (ECEC - over 10 years).

# B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

## Country and Agricultural Context:

1. Cambodia is highly vulnerable to climate change and disaster risks. It is ranked 14th out of 183 countries in the Global Climate Risk Index (CRI) 2019, which measures the impact of extreme weather events from 1997-2016. The country is also ranked one of the five least developed countries (LLDCs). Almost half of the population lives below the poverty line and 85% depend on agriculture for their livelihoods. Climate change impacts include increased frequency and intensity of droughts, floods, and storms. The agricultural sector is particularly vulnerable to climate risks due to its dependence on natural resources. The country has experienced significant economic growth in recent years, but this has not been matched by improvements in disaster risk reduction (DRR) capacity. The United Nations Office for Disaster Risk Reduction (UNISDR) estimates that just over 7% of GDP is spent on DRR.

2. Agriculture is the primary source of livelihoods for the overwhelming majority of people living in rural provinces of Cambodia and accounts for 22.5% of the country's Gross Domestic Product (GDP). The sector employs around 48% of the total labour force, with over 70% of farmers relying on rainfed agriculture. By 2050, it is estimated that climate change will reduce rice yields by up to 16%. Climate change impacts include more severe floods and droughts, which can lead to crop failure and loss of livestock. These impacts are exacerbated by poor infrastructure, limited access to credit, and lack of insurance. Livelihoods are further threatened by deforestation, land degradation, and unsustainable fishing practices.

3. Farming systems are mainly subsistence-based and remain largely rainfed, with the productivity only 50% of the global average. Yields are low due to limited use of modern inputs such as improved seeds, fertilizers, and irrigation. The sector is highly vulnerable to climate risks, including droughts, floods, and pests. Climate change impacts include more severe floods and droughts, which can lead to crop failure and loss of livestock. These impacts are exacerbated by poor infrastructure, limited access to credit, and lack of insurance. Livelihoods are further threatened by deforestation, land degradation, and unsustainable fishing practices.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V1.2 PAGE 7

These often come at the cost of past and economic repairs for farmers, because well-managed tree-based systems can provide multiple benefits to farmers and their communities. For example, agroforestry systems can improve soil fertility, reduce erosion, moderate microclimates, increase biodiversity, and enhance carbon sequestration. These systems also provide non-timber forest products (e.g., fruits, nuts, fodder) that are important for food security and nutrition. Moreover, they can generate income from the sale of timber and other forest products, which have a better reputation to food, are chosen by farmers in areas that normally would not be owned than by commercial loggers.

4. **Livelihoods**: Farmers lack the knowledge and tools to adapt their farming activities to changing climatic conditions. Climate change will exacerbate existing livelihood challenges for smallholder farmers, particularly those living in marginal environments. These farmers depend on natural resources for their survival and are vulnerable to climate variability, including droughts, floods, and changes in precipitation patterns. They also face challenges from the negative impacts of climate change, extreme weather events, and population growth.

5. **Commodities**: GHG emissions are dominated by the Forestry and Other Land Use (FOLU) sector (80% of total emissions). The FOLU sector includes deforestation and forest degradation, as well as 20% of land use emissions (GHG emissions within the FOLU framework are dominated by the agriculture sector). Commodity-driven deforestation is a major source of GHG emissions. Cattle farming accounts for 65% of all GHG emissions in Brazil, while in Indonesia, palm oil production accounts for 30%. Land use changes and the clearing of forests for agricultural purposes are the main drivers of GHG emissions. These activities also lead to biodiversity loss, carbon emissions, and ecosystem degradation.

The adoption of climate-smart agriculture practices is essential to reduce emissions, and the ability of the private sector to invest in these practices is limited by the lack of appropriate technologies and financial services. The adoption of climate-smart agriculture practices can be enhanced by the development of appropriate technologies and financial services. The adoption of climate-smart agriculture practices can also be enhanced by the development of appropriate technologies and financial services.

Climate change affects women, men, and vulnerable people differently, and their anticipated actions and responses to climate change will vary depending on their gender, age, ethnicity, and socio-economic status. Women and children are among the most vulnerable groups in society, and they are often the most affected by climate change. The adoption of climate-smart agriculture practices can help to reduce the impact of climate change on these groups.

The project area comprises six districts: Kamwenge, Kibale, Kabarole, Kyenjojo, Bundibugyo, and Ntoroko. These districts have the highest vegetation density, high soil permeability of poor households, and a high proportion of women-headed households. The region is also highly dependent on agricultural production and water availability through precipitation.

![Figure 1: Map of Project Area](image_url)

The project area was already under stress prior to the COVID-19 pandemic with small-scale farmers and local communities facing challenges such as climate change, pressure from wildlife, and changes in rainfall patterns. The dry weather and heavy precipitation during the rainy season have exacerbated these challenges. The project aims to strengthen community resilience through improved water availability in rivers and ponds. This proposed underlying investment project will support the implementation of climate-smart irrigation systems.

Since 2020, the negative impacts of climate change and the patterns have led to a 30% loss in GDP due to its impact on climate impacts, damaging global agricultural commodity prices, along with other costs and losses. The region has been identified as one of the most vulnerable regions in terms of production and consumption costs of agricultural products. This is highly correlated to the neighboring countries' vulnerability to climate change.

The project will be implemented by the Ministry of Water and Environment (MWE) and the National Climate Change Office (NCCO).

# Climate Change Impacts and Vulnerability of the Caribbean Basin

9. The Tuna Bag Lake Basin, often referred to as the core basin of Colombia, undergoes cycles of severe droughts that have a significant impact on its water resources and the surrounding ecosystems. Droughts can lead to reduced river flows, which in turn affects the availability of water for irrigation and domestic use. This has implications for agriculture and food security, particularly during periods of prolonged drought.

10. On average, annual losses due to loss of wet season are caused by floods and droughts is approximately $25 million annually. The most severe impacts occur between May and October, driven by seasonal variations in rainfall patterns. Flooding can lead to significant economic losses, including damage to infrastructure and displacement of people. Additionally, the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events have increased over time, exacerbating the impact of droughts and floods.

11. Drought has a significant social and economic impact on Colombia. In particular, the agricultural sector is highly vulnerable to drought conditions. According to the National Institute of Meteorology (INMET), between 2015 and 2016, 18 provinces in Colombia were affected by drought, with 3 million people impacted. The agricultural sector was particularly hard hit, with losses estimated at $470 million. Additionally, the tourism industry was also affected, with a loss of $250 million due to reduced tourist activity.

12. The impact of climate change on agriculture is expected to continue in the coming years, with more frequent and severe droughts and floods. This will have significant implications for food security and economic development in the region. It is important that governments and international organizations work together to address these challenges and support vulnerable communities.

### 17.
Irrigation crop and livestock systems will also experience increased risks particularly in areas where water availability is limited or where groundwater resources are already stressed. For example, the demand for irrigation water is expected to increase by about 20% (IPCC, 2000) compared to 2000, compounded by 2050. These projections are for the RCP4.5 scenario and without CO2 fertilization effects. Irrigation systems could become more vulnerable with a decreased land-to-time ratio. It is projected that agriculture will be affected by climate change in many ways (Milly et al., 2008). Our baseline scenario, head-start conditions (RCP6.0) will exceed more than 350 parts per million (ppm) CO2 equivalent by the end of this century. This is expected to have a negative impact on agriculture and food security. One of the challenges in agriculture is that it is limited by rain which can be affected by climate change. The poorest farmers are most vulnerable to changes in weather patterns and agricultural systems and technologies.

## Data from FAO Climate Adaptation in Rural Development: Assessment 1 for CARIB which explores the impact of climate change on agriculture in the Caribbean

### Sex Barriers to Climate Resilience

29. Climate analysts highlight four main risks for women: (i) a decline in the rainy season days, despite increased precipitation; (ii) increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events; (iii) increasing temperatures causing deteriorating soil conditions for many crops, and further drying the soil; and (iv) increased incidence of pests and diseases. These risks affect women's roles in farming, roofing, washing, and food preparation, leading to reduced productivity and damage to infrastructure and livelihoods.

Assessment 1: Climate Adaptation in Rural Development, Caribbean Community Climate Change Centre, 2013.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL

These funds will contribute through climate change mitigation measures that focus on biodiversity conservation and ecosystem restoration. The proposal will support the development of green infrastructure and promote sustainable land management practices. Where possible, the project will develop local supply chains but avoid and minimize adverse environmental impacts from resource extraction and farming. It will also enhance water and soil management, reduce greenhouse gas emissions, and improve livelihoods through agroforestry, silvopastoralism, and other ecosystem-based adaptation measures.

## 21. A major barrier to sustainable agriculture development is climate-related disasters that influence the agricultural sector. Measures should be taken to mitigate these risks by improving the resilience of farmers and their communities.

Most of the countries in the region are highly vulnerable to natural disasters such as floods, droughts, and hurricanes. The Department of Planning (DOP) is a key agency for disaster risk management at the national level. The DOP has developed a comprehensive National Disaster Management Plan that includes measures to mitigate the impact of climate change on agriculture. The plan focuses on improving the resilience of farmers and their communities by providing them with access to early warning systems, crop insurance, and other risk management tools.

## 22. Agriculture is one of the most vulnerable sectors to climate change. The project will support the development of climate-smart agriculture practices that enhance productivity and resilience to climate change.

The project will promote the adoption of climate-smart agriculture practices such as conservation agriculture, agroforestry, and integrated pest management. These practices will help farmers to adapt to changing climatic conditions and improve their livelihoods. The project will also support the development of early warning systems for extreme weather events and provide farmers with access to weather information and climate services.

## 23. The local private sector will be a key partner in efforts to achieve the goals set out above, both in terms of investment and job creation. Local businesses are well positioned to contribute to the achievement of these objectives through their networks and expertise.

The project will support the development of public-private partnerships (PPPs) for the establishment of climate-smart agriculture value chains. These partnerships will involve local businesses, farmers' organizations, and other stakeholders in the design and implementation of the project. The project will also provide technical assistance to local businesses to help them develop new products and services that meet the needs of farmers and consumers.

## D

### Strengthening climate resilience of local communities, ecosystems and livelihoods in Cambodia

28. **Horticulture** is one key sector that will be the focus of this proposal. It includes the ACDI/VOCA-based Integrated Agriculture Income Enhancement (IAIE) project, which has been implemented by the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries (MAFF). This project aims to modernize horticultural production systems in Cambodia through the adoption of climate-smart agriculture practices. The project will be implemented in partnership with the World Bank's Climate-Smart Agriculture Project (CSAP) in all 24 provinces in Cambodia.

### Integrated Rice Research Institute

The International Rice Research Institute (IRRI) has been working closely with the Cambodian government to promote locally specific climate-resilient rice cultivation technologies and a wide range of activities under the ECP. In addition, IRRI is also supporting the development of climate-smart agriculture practices in Cambodia through its Climate-Smart Agriculture (CSA) project. The CSA project is also being implemented in partnership with the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries (MAFF). The project aims to enhance the resilience of smallholder farmers to climate change by promoting climate-smart agriculture practices.

### Climate Adaptation and Sustainable Agriculture for Resilience

The Climate Adaptation and Sustainable Agriculture for Resilience (CASAR) project is focusing on climate change adaptation through sustainable farming. The project aims to increase the resilience of smallholder farmers in Cambodia to climate change by promoting climate-smart agriculture practices. The project will be implemented in partnership with the World Bank's Climate-Smart Agriculture Project (CSAP).

## Pathway 2: Irrigation Schemes Level

38. Much of the irrigation infrastructure in Caribbean is outdated and in need of rehabilitation and upgrading. The rehabilitation of these schemes will require significant investment. The high cost of energy, particularly diesel, has led to the use of solar pumps for irrigation. The high salinity levels which aggravate the impact of climate risk. The Theory of Change (ToC) identifies the need to strengthen the capacity and sustainability of the systems, protect the natural capital, reduce greenhouse gas emissions, and enhance water use efficiency. In addition, the project will improve the capacity of WVICS to water resources management through improved data collection and monitoring. This will lead to better planning and management of water resources. The most efficient solutions for irrigation are solar pumps which can be used in combination with drip irrigation systems.

## Pathway 3: Institutional and Enabling Environment Level

39. Caribbean can be transformed through strategic investments in strengthening soft capacity for integrated approaches to infrastructure design and planning, including the development of climate-smart infrastructure. This will require a strong enabling environment and adequate systems, strengthened by policy and regulatory frameworks for climate action. The Theory of Change (ToC) identifies the need to strengthen the institutional capacity and sustainability of institutions that support integrated and sustainable solutions for management and operation and maintenance of water resources. These institutions will make them more aware of these opportunities and open the way for innovation in the long term.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V.1.0 | PAGE 19

## Component 5: Sub-national Climate Action

### 40.
The project's results will be achieved through the implementation of three components. The first component focuses on strengthening climate resilience and adaptation to climate change impacts by implementing selected climate schemes and their sub-schemes, including the provision of technical assistance for the development and implementation of these schemes. The second component will focus on building capacity in climate finance and enhancing the enabling environment for climate action through proof of concept activities that strengthen the sustainability of water resource systems in both urban and rural areas. This includes capacity building and re-orientation of farmers' production practices and assets to both improved water management and climate-smart agriculture. The third component will focus on institutional strengthening of the National Designated Authority (NDA) and its sub-national counterparts, including the establishment of a Climate Finance Academy. This component will also support the NDA in its role as the focal point for the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) process and will provide technical assistance to the NDA and its sub-national counterparts.

### 41.
The project aims to comprise 2,250 households. The scheme of sub-projects under the project will include the following: the use of Payments for Ecosystem Services (PES), Land Use Change, and Climate-Smart Agriculture (CSA) approaches in the target areas. The project will also support the development of climate-smart agriculture practices that are resilient to climate change impacts. This includes the promotion of sustainable farming practices such as agroforestry, conservation agriculture, and integrated pest management systems.

# e-Voucher Diagram - CAISAI

## Key Features of CAISAI
1. **CAISAI** provides a user-friendly interface for farmers to access agricultural services and products.
2. **Mobile platforms** enable farmers to use their mobile phones to access these services.
3. **Microfinance service providers** offer financial services tailored to the needs of smallholder farmers.

## CAISAI
- Key features include:
  - User-friendly interface
  - Access to agricultural services and products

## Mobile Platforms
- Features:
  - Accessible via mobile phones
  - Integration with microfinance services

## Microfinance Service Providers
- Features:
  - Tailored financial services for smallholder farmers
  - Integration with CAISAI and mobile platforms

## Products
- Key features include:
  - Performance indicators
  - Monitoring and evaluation (M&E) systems
  - Data collection tools
  - Feedback mechanisms

### Activity 1.6 Community-based Monitoring and Evaluation (CBME) of the Implementation (NCDO-S)
56. **Objective**: To ensure that the CAISAI system is implemented effectively, it is essential to monitor and evaluate its performance in providing services to farmers.
   - **Community-based monitoring** involves local communities in evaluating the effectiveness of the CAISAI system.
   - **Performance indicators** will be developed to assess the system's impact on farmers' livelihoods and agricultural practices.
   - **Data collection tools** will be designed to gather information from farmers, enabling the evaluation of the system's performance.

57. **Implementation**: The CBME approach will involve training local communities in monitoring techniques. This can facilitate the integration of feedback into the CAISAI system, allowing for continuous improvement. Data collected through this process will be used to inform decision-making at both the sub-national and national levels.
   - **Feedback mechanisms** will enable farmers to provide input on the system's performance.
   - **Training programs** will be developed to ensure that local communities have the skills needed to effectively monitor the CAISAI system.

61. The program will establish Multi-Stakeholder Platforms (MSPs) to facilitate collaboration among value chain actors. Scoping on financing opportunities and improving methodologies where needed, technical assistance, and capacity building for smallholders will be provided. This activity will also aim to support the development of innovative financing mechanisms for smallholder farmers as well as support the development of climate-smart value chains.

62. The D4D’s will provide a structured approach to identify challenges and opportunities with the value chain actors. The program will identify key stakeholders in the value chain, including financial institutions, input suppliers, and output buyers. The program will also aim to develop innovative financing mechanisms for smallholder farmers as well as support the development of climate-smart value chains.

## Activity 1.2.3: Public-Private Partnership (PPP) and Leveraging of Lease (INCD0-G)

65. Carbon's structural factor has experienced substantial growth over the past few decades, largely due to significant industrialization and improved food security. However, climate change poses threats to the stability of the agricultural sector. The Government of Ghana (GoG) is actively promoting sustainable farming practices that are resilient to climate change. This includes the adoption of climate-smart agriculture (CSA), which aims to increase productivity and income while reducing greenhouse gas emissions.

66. The Public-Private Partnership (PPP) Family (MPF) is designed to strengthen cross, rural, and medium-sized enterprises through partnerships between the public and private sectors. Through this partnership, the GoG encourages smallholder farmers to adopt sustainable farming practices that support their livelihoods. This initiative aims to improve food security and reduce poverty by promoting sustainable agriculture.

67. The GoG has developed a comprehensive plan for implementing climate-smart agriculture (CSA) through various programs and projects. These include the provision of training, technical assistance, and financial support to smallholder farmers. The GoG also provides access to credit and other financial services to help farmers adopt CSA practices. This initiative aims to improve food security and reduce poverty by promoting sustainable agriculture.

68. Building on IFAD's ongoing projects, such as AIM and SAAARIT, the initiative will encourage the financial sector to provide loans to smallholder farmers. The GoG will also promote the use of climate-smart technologies through training programs and workshops. This initiative aims to improve food security and reduce poverty by promoting sustainable agriculture.

69. The GoG has developed a comprehensive plan for implementing climate-smart agriculture (CSA) through various programs and projects. The GoG also provides access to credit and other financial services to help farmers adopt CSA practices. This initiative aims to improve food security and reduce poverty by promoting sustainable agriculture.

This initiative aims to improve food security and reduce poverty by promoting sustainable agriculture.

# Activity 1.3.3 Build the capacities to deliver services (NCCD-8)

T1: Capture demand from both from enhancing the beneficial data necessary for climate data collection and from the users of the data. This will be achieved through a combination of targeted outreach, stakeholder analysis, and engagement with existing user groups. The initiative will provide local group discussions and feedback on current services to identify new opportunities for service enhancement. The initiative will also enhance the capacity of the National Climate Data Centre (NCDC) to deliver climate information and data value-added services through improved communication channels.

Activity 1.3.4 Establish the agro-meteorology information and warning systems and the mechanisms including

T1: The dissemination of agri-meteorological services will be structured as a daily, bi-weekly and/or weekly bulletin to provide timely and relevant information to farmers and other stakeholders. Such bulletins will be disseminated in hard copy and also through FM radio, mobile phones, and other media. The project will leverage various methods such as the use of the [VOWRAT](https://www.vowrat.com/) as well as local government platforms. Additional activities will be undertaken through community radio stations to ensure that farmers have access to timely information on weather forecasts and climate change impacts. Special attention will be paid to include the most vulnerable groups, such as women farmers, children, and farmers with disabilities (PWD), who are often excluded from traditional media channels.

T2: An enhanced system for agro-meteorological advice, an effective delivery mechanism will be established through partnerships and cross-departmental collaboration. This initiative aims at integrating climate information into agricultural planning and decision-making processes. The initiative will also provide training on the use of agro-meteorological services to farmers and other stakeholders in order to enhance their capacity to use these services effectively.

Output 1.4: Increased existence of farm-based infrastructures to climate change (NCCD-9)

The project will provide a wide range of support to smallholder farmers, including the provision of climate-smart agricultural inputs and practices. This includes the provision of improved seeds, fertilizers, and other inputs that are resilient to climate change. The initiative will also promote the adoption of climate-resilient farming practices such as conservation agriculture, agroforestry, and integrated pest management (IPM). These practices will be promoted through farmer field schools and other training programs. The project will also provide support for the establishment of community-based early warning systems that can alert farmers to impending weather events and other climate-related risks.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal V.1.0

## T4: Readiness Support for Improved Land Tenure and Resource Rights

Readiness support will enable the government and local communities to change land use, including increasing forest cover and drought resistance, and to improve water management and drought resilience. Drought ordinances significantly affect unpaid labor, leading to higher levels of poverty and hunger. The project will promote improved land tenure and resource rights through climate-smart designs that enhance ecosystem services and support sustainable livelihoods.

### T4.A: Set up a pilot farm model

A set of pilot farm models have been developed with feasibility studies completed under Phase 2. These include the use of climate-smart agriculture, agroforestry, and integrated water management systems. The pilot farms will be used to demonstrate best practices in sustainable land management.

## Activity 1.4.1: Identification, selection, and detailed planning (ICDD-1)

The initial planning will involve consultative meetings with local communities and stakeholders to gather information on existing land tenure issues and resource rights. This will include using the Community Participation Framework (CPF) 2018 prepared under the previous phase.

## Activity 1.4.2: Technical survey and design considerations, preparation of cost estimation (ICDD-2)

Designs in housing and drought use will be developed. The handling of land for production will include the development of a detailed plan to ensure that the pilot farm is designed to meet the needs of the community. To achieve this objective, design methodologies will incorporate local knowledge and practices.

## Activity 1.4.3: Prepare 90% Drawings of farm models (ICDD-3)

The technical team will prepare detailed drawings for the pilot farm model. This includes preparing local connectors and tailoring the designs to meet the needs of the community. The final design will be prepared by the consultants.

## Activity 1.4.4: Hiring of Consultants and Experts (ICDD-5)

The hiring process will be handled under the Ministry of Rural Development (MRD). The MRD will ensure that the consultants are hired through a competitive bidding process. The consultants will be responsible for providing technical support to the project.

## Activity 1.4.5: Training on Climate-Smart Agriculture (ICDD-6)

The training will be conducted by the Ministry of Agriculture and Irrigation (MOAI). The MOAI will provide training on climate-smart agriculture practices, including the use of drought-resistant crops and sustainable farming techniques.

## Component 1

Component 1 is linked with Component 1 such that it facilitates the implementation of G1 on farm management and water use efficiency. It also provides a platform for the development of integrated water and soil management strategies to improve high efficiency smallholder irrigated agriculture systems for coping with increasing food demand.

### Outcome 2

The expected outcome of this component is that within the 6 irrigation schemes, the water management practices will be improved through the introduction of improved water management technologies. The water use efficiency in the dry season and off season is the key outcome. As concluded during the inception phase, the main challenges are the poor maintenance and operation of the irrigation and drainage infrastructure systems. This component will address these issues by improving the water use efficiency in the dry season to the level provided during the wet season. In around 70% of the total planned area, the water management practices will be improved through the introduction of improved water management technologies. The main challenges are the poor maintenance and operation of the irrigation and drainage infrastructure systems.

# Output 2.2: Flood-proofing and drainage improvement (MOWRAM)

## SI.1 Focus on improving disaster prevention and protection in farmlands and assets by establishment of early warning systems and community-based disaster risk management (CBDRRM) and new farming practices will be promoted based on the results of the hydrological modeling and consultation.

### Activity 2.2.1 Structural analysis, field surveys and preparation of plans for system upgrading (MOWRAM)
- This output focuses on equipping the 6 schemes with facilities to be included in the currently annually conducted structural analysis and maintenance.
- The focus is on the improvement of flood prevention and protection of farmlands and assets by the establishment of flood protection structures, drainage systems and flood embankments.
- This includes the study of the area where the scheme is located and the preparation of plans for the schemes to which new project activities can be added.
- Existing damage to the farming community will be repaired and the improvement of the hydrological modeling and consultation will be established along with the upgrade areas.
- Also, a study on the impact of climate change on the flood protection system and the need to improve the existing system wherever necessary to prevent excess drainage after the outer area.

## Activity 2.2.2 Strengthening and construction of flood control and drainage infrastructures (MOWRAM)
- This output focuses on strengthening and constructing flood control and drainage infrastructure in the 6 schemes.
- The focus is on the improvement of flood prevention and protection of farmlands and assets by the establishment of flood protection structures, drainage systems and flood embankments.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.1: PAGE 3

## D

### Efficiencies and Synergies

To efficiently carry out their duties, Regional Offices (ROs) should be well-resourced to ensure effective implementation of the GCF. This includes adequate funding for staffing, training, change management and transformation and operations and program delivery. The ROs will also need to have the capacity to provide technical assistance to developing countries in the design and implementation of projects and programs that are consistent with the objectives of the GCF. The ROs should also ensure the promotion of robust and inclusive stakeholder engagement for project development, implementation and monitoring.

### 8.4 Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)

#### Accredited Entity (AE)

The International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) has a long history of implementing climate change adaptation projects in the agriculture sector. IFAD is a UN agency with a mandate to invest in rural people. It works with developing countries and civil society organizations to reduce poverty and hunger. IFAD's experience in project implementation, including its ability to provide technical assistance and supervision through project design and implementation, ensures the quality of project monitoring, as well as the integrity and quality of reporting to GCF and other donors. IFAD has a strong track record of implementing projects that are consistent with the objectives of the GCF. IFAD's experience in project implementation is complemented by its extensive network of field offices and country-based staff who can provide technical assistance and supervision throughout the project cycle.

#### Executing Entity

AE, as an executing agency, i.e., a subsidiary entity with the host Government of Cambodia, using through the MEF. The Executing entity will be the host Government of Cambodia, which will be responsible for the implementation of the project. The executing agency will be the Ministry of Economy and Finance (MEF) of Cambodia. The MEF is responsible for the overall management of the project and will ensure that all financial and technical requirements are met.

#### Project Overview and Steering Arrangements

The project will be implemented by the GCF, which will be managed by a multi-disciplinary team. The core of the GCF will be a multi-disciplinary team (MDT) that provides strategic oversight over the project's implementation. The MDT will include representatives from the Ministry of Environment and Forestry (MEF), the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries (MAFF), the Ministry of Environment (MOE), and the Provincial Governors' Offices of Pursat.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.1.1 PAGE 9

## The Proposal

The proposal is submitted to transform the same behavior in their production decisions and consumption patterns through a combination of incentives and disincentives. The GCF will provide direct access to accredited entities and will support the development of innovative financing mechanisms. Beneficiaries are expected to participate actively in the implementation process, which will be monitored by the GCF Secretariat. The GCF will also promote the integration of gender equality, human rights, and social inclusion principles into its projects. The GCF will play an active role in achieving sustainable development goals (SDGs) and will support the systems through permanent GCF funding as stipulated in the agreement.

### The Flow of Funds

1. **GCF REMs** - The Green Climate Fund (GCF) is the main source of funds for the Adaptation Fund (AF). The GCF will provide direct access to accredited entities and will support the development of innovative financing mechanisms.
2. **PAADF** - The Pan-African Agency for Development Fund (PAADF) is a regional development finance institution that provides funding to African countries for infrastructure, agriculture, and other projects.
3. **AF** - The Adaptation Fund (AF) is a fund established by the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) to assist developing countries in adapting to the adverse effects of climate change.

The PAADF will provide funding to the AF, which will then be used to support projects that are aligned with the objectives of the GCF. The PAADF will also provide technical assistance and capacity building to the AF to ensure that it is able to effectively implement its projects.

### MEF

MEF will authorize MOU/RAI and the NCDDS PIs to open designated accounts (DAAs) at the National Bank of Commerce (NBC). These accounts will be used to receive funds from the PAADF and the GCF. The MEF will also provide technical assistance to the NCDDS PIs to ensure that they are able to effectively manage their funds.

![Figure 6: Funds Flow Diagram (URB)](image_url)

- **GCF REMs** - $10M
- **PAADF** - $5M
- **AF** - $2.5M

#### Step-by-Step Flow:

1. **Step 1:** GCF REMs ($10M) to PAADF.
   - **Sub-step 1.1:** PAADF ($5M) to AF.
   - **Sub-step 1.2:** PAADF ($5M) to NCDDS PIs.

2. **Step 2:** GCF REMs ($10M) to AF.
   - **Sub-step 2.1:** AF ($2.5M) to NCDDS PIs.
   - **Sub-step 2.2:** AF ($7.5M) to MEF for technical assistance.

3. **Step 3:** MEF provides technical assistance and capacity building to NCDDS PIs.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal

## 8.3 Justification for GCF funding request

The current proposal for GCF funding is aligned with regional and national priorities which are essential to achieving the goals of the Green Climate Fund. There is need to mobilize financial resources for climate change adaptation and mitigation in order to address the adverse effects of climate change. The estimated migration works is estimated to be done US$ 1 billion plus US$ 2 billion for all plantation sectors, with many development projects and investment needs. One of these priorities is implementing the current project which will help to reduce the vulnerability of the population to natural disasters. The project will also help to improve the livelihoods of the most vulnerable countries to the impacts of climate change such as already facing GCP green climate fund. The project will also help to improve the livelihoods of the most vulnerable countries to the impacts of climate change such as already facing GCP green climate fund.

### Project benefits and economic benefits: Total cost USD 3 billion

- **Project benefits**: The project will provide significant benefits to the local community, including improved infrastructure, increased access to clean water and sanitation, and enhanced food security.
- **Economic benefits**: The project is expected to create jobs and stimulate economic growth in the region. It will also help to reduce greenhouse gas emissions and contribute to global efforts to combat climate change.

The need is urgent to ensure that local communities will be required in the project design. Cambodia is regarded as one of the fastest-growing economies in Southeast Asia with an average annual growth rate of 7% by 2030.

The public sector's financial resources are limited, and the private sector's financial resources are also limited. ## 127.
The project will capitalize on the existing Canalization with law under which the farmers have is conducive to the use of irrigation systems and this requirement is well accepted although at a higher cost. The project will be implemented through the Department of Irrigation, Maine and Water (DMW) in collaboration with the Ministry of Agriculture (MOA). The project will be funded by the Government of Uganda (GoU), the World Bank (WB) and the Green Climate Fund (GCF). It is expected that the project will be implemented over a period of 5 years. The project will be implemented through the following steps:

- **Step 1:** The project will be designed to enhance ownership and participation of local beneficiaries communities which will play their role in system cleaning and maintenance.
- **Step 2:** To ensure the longest possible sustainability, the project will be designed to operate as a self-sustaining function during both dry and wet seasons.

## 128.
During implementation monitoring, MOWWM will be assisted in developing a post-project strategy for continuous monitoring of the irrigation system. This will include the operation and maintenance of the irrigation systems. The project will also develop a plan to ensure that the irrigation system is maintained after the end of the project period. The project will also develop a plan to ensure that the irrigation system is maintained after the end of the project period.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL

## Project Objectives and Expected Results

The project expects significant yield and crop productivity improvements in a targeted region subject to climate change impacts through the adoption of climate-smart WAPM based integrated production systems. The project will promote sustainable intensification of agriculture, improve livelihoods, and enhance food security and nutrition for smallholder farmers. The project will also contribute to reducing greenhouse gas emissions from agriculture by promoting carbon sequestration in soils and enhancing soil fertility.

### Expected Results

The use of extended duration maize varieties, improved fallows, and the optimal timing of inputs will increase yields significantly. The adoption of climate-smart practices such as conservation agriculture (CA) will improve soil health and water retention capacity. The project will also promote the adoption of agroforestry systems to enhance carbon sequestration and biodiversity.

### Project Implementation

The project is expected to impact 562,710 people directly and 1,151,815 people indirectly (Table 2). The direct beneficiaries are smallholder farmers who will adopt climate-smart practices. The project will also benefit local communities by creating jobs and improving infrastructure.

### Project Activities

The project activities include:

- Training and capacity building for farmers on climate-smart agriculture
- Promotion of improved maize varieties, fallows, and agroforestry systems
- Establishment of demonstration plots to showcase the benefits of climate-smart practices
- Provision of inputs such as seeds, fertilizers, and tools to farmers
- Development of market linkages for farmers' produce

### Project Budget

The project budget is US$ 32,058,947 out of a total amount of $10,000,000. The largest share of the budget (60%) will be used for capacity building and training activities. The remaining 40% will be used for inputs, infrastructure development, and monitoring and evaluation.

### Project Duration

The project duration is five years, starting from January 1, 2023, to December 31, 2027.

### Project Management

The project will be managed by a team of experienced professionals with expertise in climate change adaptation, agriculture, and rural development. The project management unit will be based in the targeted region and will work closely with local communities to ensure the successful implementation of the project.

### Project Monitoring and Evaluation

The project will have a robust monitoring and evaluation system to track progress towards achieving its objectives. The system will include regular field visits, annual reports, and periodic evaluations by independent experts.

### Project Sustainability

The project aims to promote sustainable practices that will continue beyond the project duration. The project will work closely with local communities to ensure that the benefits of the project are sustained in the long term.

## D

Embracing the private sector will unlock significant opportunities. The public majority owns is to ensure that the benefits of these investments are shared with all Canadians. The Government of Canada supports a range of initiatives, including:

- supporting MEEAIs in other high-value regions (dairy, beef, and poultry)
- improving productivity through better management practices
- enhancing learning technologies, including through dairy models
- investing in research and development to improve the sustainability of farming practices

Another key strategy involves MEEAIs in Canada. These investments will support the adoption of best practices by farmers and ranchers, including:

- energy-efficient buildings
- renewable energy systems
- improved water management practices

These investments will also support the development of new technologies that can be adopted by other producers. This includes the development of precision agriculture models, which can help to reduce input costs and improve sustainability.

### D.4. Needs and Risks

#### D.4.1. Needs

The private sector is a critical component of Canada's economy and contributes significantly to job creation. Carbon pricing will have a significant impact on the competitiveness of high-emitting sectors. The Government of Canada is committed to ensuring that these investments are made in a way that supports the long-term sustainability of the economy.

#### D.4.2. Risks

Carbon pricing can be a catalyst for new jobs and investment, but it also presents risks. These include:

- the potential for job losses in high-emitting sectors
- the impact on competitiveness of Canadian industries
- the risk of carbon leakage (where companies move their operations to countries with lower carbon prices)

The Government of Canada is committed to mitigating these risks through a range of measures, including:

- providing support to workers and communities affected by job losses
- investing in innovation and clean technology
- working with provinces and territories to ensure that the benefits of carbon pricing are shared across the country

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V.1.1 PAGE 8

Meeting these targets: Picking development-focused interventions taking place in upstream catchments to reduce sediment and nutrient loads entering the river is a priority. The Green Climate Fund (GCF) has been identified as a potential source of funding for this purpose.

## Agriculture
Climate change and increased flood frequency via direct and indirect impacts on crop growth processes. Direct impacts include alterations to carbon dioxide availability, precipitation levels, temperature, and soil moisture content. Indirect effects are due to changes in pest and disease prevalence, nutrient cycling, and water availability. These changes can affect agricultural productivity and food security. Climate change also affects the livelihoods of farmers through increased risks from extreme weather events such as droughts, floods, and storms. Adaptation measures include improved crop varieties, better irrigation systems, and climate-smart agriculture practices. However, these solutions are often expensive and require significant investment.

### Livelihoods
Livestock farming is a major source of income for many communities in the region. Climate change affects livestock productivity through changes in pasture quality and availability, water scarcity, and increased disease prevalence. Droughts can lead to reduced forage quality and quantity, while heatwaves can cause heat stress in livestock. These impacts can lead to decreased milk production and lower meat yields. Adaptation measures include improved grazing management, drought-resistant forage species, and better animal health practices.

## Poverty and Inequality
Poverty reduction in Cambodia has been rapid, yet disease prevalence remains high. The socio-economic impact of climate change is exacerbated by the lack of access to healthcare services and poor sanitation facilities. Climate change affects the poorest groups in society, particularly farmers and rural communities, who are most vulnerable to extreme weather events such as floods and droughts. These impacts can lead to increased food insecurity, reduced agricultural productivity, and decreased livelihood opportunities. The poorest households are often unable to afford climate-resilient housing or infrastructure, which increases their vulnerability to climate change.

### Health
The health impacts of climate change in Cambodia include an increase in the prevalence of vector-borne diseases such as malaria and dengue fever. Climate change also affects the availability and quality of water resources, which can lead to increased waterborne diseases. The poorest households are often unable to afford adequate housing or infrastructure to protect themselves from these impacts. This increases their vulnerability to climate change.

## Financial Constraints
The updated NDC has estimated that the total funding required for at least some of the proposed interventions is USD 10 million per year over a ten-year period. This funding will be used to support the implementation of adaptation and mitigation measures, as well as to build capacity and enhance knowledge management.

## D

The technologies and adopt the practices that can build resilience. CAAIRAR will directly large scale investments in climate-smart agriculture to increase agricultural productivity, improve food security and nutrition, and the sustainable management of natural resources.

### Gender: In Canada, women who are mainly subsistence farmers have been identified as the most vulnerable to climate change. They have less access to credit, land, and other productive resources in the same areas where they live. Women also face greater challenges in accessing markets for their products due to lack of infrastructure and transportation. CAAIRAR will support women's empowerment through the provision of agricultural inputs, training, and financial services.

### Youth: Youth are an important part of the agricultural sector. They have the potential to contribute significantly to agricultural development and food security. CAAIRAR will support youth engagement in agriculture by providing them with access to credit, land, and other productive resources. CAAIRAR will also provide training and mentoring opportunities for young farmers.

### Indigenous Peoples: Indigenous peoples are an important part of the agricultural sector. They have a unique relationship with the land and have developed traditional practices that can help build resilience to climate change. CAAIRAR will support indigenous peoples' engagement in agriculture by providing them with access to credit, land, and other productive resources. CAAIRAR will also provide training and mentoring opportunities for indigenous farmers.

### Health: The health of the agricultural sector is closely linked to the health of the environment. CAAIRAR will support efforts to improve food safety, reduce the use of hazardous chemicals, and promote sustainable agriculture practices. CAAIRAR will also support efforts to improve access to healthcare services for farmers and their families.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal

## Allocation Plans (NAPs): The Government and updated is Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs)

This section provides an overview of the allocation plans for the Green Climate Fund (GCF). This effort is supported by the NDC Partnership and is in line with the priorities set out in the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) process. The plan aims to enhance climate resilience, reduce greenhouse gas emissions, and promote sustainable development.

### Key Objectives

- **Climate Resilience**: Enhance climate resilience through improved early warning systems for extreme weather events.
- **Emissions Reduction**: Support initiatives that contribute to the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions.
- **Sustainable Development**: Promote sustainable development by integrating climate change considerations into national policies and strategies.

### Funding Priorities

The funding will focus on the following areas:

1. **Climate Resilience**: Enhance climate resilience through improved early warning systems for extreme weather events.
2. **Emissions Reduction**: Support initiatives that contribute to the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions.
3. **Sustainable Development**: Promote sustainable development by integrating climate change considerations into national policies and strategies.

### Implementation Strategy

The implementation strategy includes:

- **National Adaptation Plan (NAP)**: The NAP process is a nationally owned process to identify medium- and long-term adaptation needs, and to develop and implement strategies for addressing those needs.
- **NDC Partnership**: Collaboration with the NDC Partnership to enhance climate resilience and reduce emissions.
- **Early Warning Systems**: Development of early warning systems to mitigate the impact of extreme weather events.

### Monitoring and Evaluation

A robust monitoring and evaluation framework will be established, including:

- **Management Information System (MIS)**: A system to track progress and ensure accountability.
- **Regular Reporting**: Regular reporting on progress towards achieving climate resilience and emissions reduction targets.
- **Stakeholder Engagement**: Continuous engagement with stakeholders to ensure transparency and accountability.

### Existing Initiatives

The country has placed a strong emphasis on policy development, including:

- **Climate Change Act (CCA)**: The CCA provides the legal framework for climate change mitigation and adaptation in the country.
- **National Adaptation Plan (NAP)**: The NAP is a nationally owned process to identify medium- and long-term adaptation needs.
- **Early Warning Systems**: Development of early warning systems to mitigate the impact of extreme weather events.

### Conclusion

The Green Climate Fund allocation plan aims to enhance climate resilience, reduce greenhouse gas emissions, and promote sustainable development. Through collaboration with international partners and a focus on national priorities, the country is well-positioned to achieve its climate goals.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal

## Principles of Green Growth

In addition to the existing building key indicators will incorporate principles of green growth. The Long-Term Strategy for Climate Neutrality (LTSCN 2050) aims to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by at least 80% compared to 1990 levels and to promote climate neutrality by 2050.

The strategy is based on the following key principles:

- The use of renewable energy sources.
- The promotion of energy efficiency.
- The protection and sustainable management of forests, biodiversity, and ecosystems.
- The support for a healthy and resilient economy that is competitive and socially inclusive.
- The support for a just transition to a climate-neutral society.

## Existing GCF Country Programmes

GCF has been supporting Cambodia since 2015 on the green development agenda. At present, GCF has already funded three major projects with the total amount of USD 34 million. These are:

- **Pulses-Special-Phase Partnership for Economically Sound Agriculture and Resilient Livelihoods in Cambodia (PSP-PHASE)**.
- **PEARL: MAU - alignment of all CIFS activities with one of the existing project**.
- **Learning agenda of UDO 10 million have been spent and USD 3.5 million have been disbursed**.

## The Royal Government of Cambodia (RGC)

The Royal Government of Cambodia (RGC) is committed to the sustainable development of green growth. Existing sustainable programs are key priority for Cambodia to achieve both economic and social development goals. The RGC has initiated several initiatives such as:

- **Integrated Water Resource Management (IWRM)**.
- **National Adaptation Plan (NAP)**.
- **Climate Change Master Plan (CCMP)**.

The RGC is working with the GCF to support the CARE project and other projects. The RGC has been leading the process with which additional resources are needed for climate change adaptation and mitigation. The RGC has also closed sourcing and getting the necessary assistance in the implementation of the project.

## Commitment to Gender Equality and Alignment to National Development Policies

Indicating the partnership, the Royal Government of Cambodia has adopted a program approach to addressing gender considerations.

## D

will very positively action starting gender initiatives. Gender equality is a priority sector for GCF and is an important element of the climate change agenda. The GCF’s gender policy is based on the importance of strengthening institutional capacity to mainstream gender into all aspects of operations. The Medium-Term Strategy (2015-2019) is premised on the global need to address gender equality in the planning, implementation and monitoring of climate finance. Gender equality and women’s empowerment are key priorities for GCF. Gender equality is a cross-cutting theme that requires action at all levels of the organization. The GCF Secretariat has been working with the Global Green Growth Institute (GGGI) on a project to strengthen the monitoring and evaluation systems of the GCF-funded projects. This project is aligned with GGGI’s mission to support developing countries in achieving their green growth objectives. The project aims to enhance the capacity of the GCF Secretariat to monitor and evaluate gender equality outcomes from the projects it funds. The project will also provide technical assistance to the GCF-accredited entities to strengthen their monitoring and evaluation systems for gender equality outcomes. The project is expected to be completed in 2021.

# 102 Local Communities and the Private Sector

In order to make the investments viable, there is a strong case for local communities such as First Nations, provinces, municipalities and private investors to be involved in the development of renewable energy projects. This would allow them to benefit from the economic opportunities that these projects provide. It is expected that First Nations will have to establish effective partnerships with the private sector to ensure that they are able to participate fully in the development and operation of these projects. The private sector can also play a key role in establishing effective partnerships with local communities, which would allow them to benefit from the economic opportunities that these projects provide.

# 103 Cost-Benefit Analysis of the Migration and Adaptation Outcomes

GOF is being requested to undertake a cost-benefit analysis (CBA) of the migration and adaptation outcomes. The GOF is expected to consider the following:

- The costs and benefits of the migration and adaptation outcomes for the 562,757 people directly and -1.5 million indirectly affected by climate change.
- The costs and benefits of the migration and adaptation outcomes for the 480,000 people who are expected to be displaced by sea-level rise.
- The costs and benefits of the migration and adaptation outcomes for the 200,000 people who are expected to be displaced by drought.
- The costs and benefits of the migration and adaptation outcomes for the 150,000 people who are expected to be displaced by wildfires.
- The costs and benefits of the migration and adaptation outcomes for the 100,000 people who are expected to be displaced by heatwaves.

# 104 Economics and Financial Rate of Return

The economic and financial analysis of the project consists of an assessment of the potential economic and social impacts of the project. The assessment is based on a cost-benefit analysis (CBA) which is used to determine the net present value (NPV) of the project. The NPV is calculated by subtracting the discounted costs from the discounted benefits of the project. The CBA is used to assess the feasibility of the project and to determine whether or not it is likely to be profitable.

The economic analysis is conducted using a discount rate of 7% per annum and the 10-year benefit-cost ratio (BCR) is calculated as follows:

- The net present value (NPV) of the project is calculated by subtracting the discounted costs from the discounted benefits.
- The BCR is calculated by dividing the NPV by the initial investment cost.

## Leverage and Credit: The GCF grant will be used for co-financing to leverage additional resources from other sources. In addition, GCF funds will be matched by a combination of domestic and international sources. The GCF grant will be matched by 50 percent from the government (10 percent) and 50 percent from private sector sources (40 percent). The GCF grant will also leverage long-term financing for future investments.

## Implementation of Private and Concessional Financing: Concessional funds are expected to be used in the project. The concessional funds will be used to finance the capital costs of the project, which is expected to be around 40 percent of the total cost. The concessional funds will also be used to finance the operating expenses of the project.

# E. LOGICAL FRAMEWORK

The objective here is to align the project/programme and its components with the GCF Integrated Results Management Framework to assess the potential impact of the project/programme.

## E.1 Project/Programme Focus

Projects should be designed to address the needs of a specific or additional programme. For more complex programmes, additional focus areas may be included (e.g., increased resilience adaptation).

### E.2 Impact level: Paragraph short potential (max 600 words, approximately 1-2 pages):

#### Assessment

| Description | Current state (baseline) | Potential target scenario |
|-------------|--------------------------|---------------------------|
| **Economic** | The lack of access to finance and the limited capacity of local communities to develop sustainable livelihoods has led to the degradation of natural resources. | Enhanced economic opportunities, improved livelihoods, and increased resilience through sustainable economic activities at the community level. |
| **Social**   | The project will focus on improving social cohesion and reducing conflict among different ethnic groups. | Increased access to early warning systems, improved health services, and better education facilities for all communities. |
| **Environmental** | Deforestation and land degradation have led to a decrease in biodiversity and an increase in greenhouse gas emissions. | Sustainable management of the natural resources such as forests, water bodies, and agricultural lands due to increased awareness and capacity building. |

#### How the project/programme will be implemented

The project will focus on the training of 4500 local farmers and community members in sustainable farming practices and climate-smart agriculture techniques. This project aims to leverage $75,000 annually for the next five years. The project also aims to train 60 local extension workers who will be responsible for providing ongoing support and training to the farmers.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal v.1.0 | PAGE 82

## Government

Government should undertake to:

- Provide a supportive environment for climate change adaptation and mitigation.
- Ensure that policies and programs are consistent with national climate change strategies.

## Resilience

While there is a need to enhance the resilience of agriculture systems, it is also important to:

- Understand the risks associated with climate change.
- Develop appropriate risk management strategies.
- Build capacity to adapt to climate change.

The sustainability of the project will be enhanced by:

- Ensuring that the project is consistent with national policies and strategies.
- Building on existing initiatives and experiences.
- Encouraging local ownership and participation in the project.

## Sustainability

Development of a model for climate-smart agriculture through pilot projects. The project will:

- Provide technical assistance to farmers.
- Develop and implement climate-smart practices.
- Build capacity to adapt to climate change.

Platform will use the development of climate-smart agriculture as an entry point to:

- Promote sustainable agricultural practices.
- Encourage the adoption of climate-smart practices by smallholder farmers.
- Support the integration of climate-smart practices into national policies and strategies.

The project will focus on building the capacity of farmers, particularly women, to:

- Adopt climate-smart practices.
- Improve their livelihoods through increased agricultural productivity.
- Build resilience to climate change.

Close integration of the project into the national climate change strategy is essential. Annual performance reviews will be conducted by the National Adaptation Committee and Resource Initiative Secretariat. The project will be implemented in collaboration with relevant ministries, departments, and other stakeholders.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.1 | PAGE 84

## project scenario

The GCF Readiness Programme on additional financing for climate change mitigation is implemented by UNDP in collaboration with IPPCC and other partners. The project aims to support the implementation of Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) that have been submitted as part of the Paris Agreement, and to endorse the development of new NDCs. The project will help countries to develop their own climate change mitigation strategies and to implement them in a manner consistent with national priorities.

## Reduction of GHG emissions

1. **Methodological guidelines for the preparation of Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) under the Paris Agreement**
   - 2018: First edition of the NDC guide published by UNFCCC.
   - 2020: Second edition of the NDC guide published, incorporating feedback from countries and other stakeholders.
   - 2021: Third edition of the NDC guide published, including updates on new developments in climate change mitigation.

2. **Alignment with Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs)**
   - Countries will develop their own NDCs in accordance with the Paris Agreement.
   - The GCF will provide funding for alignment activities that support countries' NDCs.
   - The GCF will also provide technical assistance to help countries implement their NDCs.

3. **Capacity building**
   - The GCF will provide capacity-building support to countries to help them develop and implement their NDCs.
   - This support will include training, technical assistance, and other forms of capacity-building activities.

4. **Monitoring and reporting**
   - Countries will be required to monitor and report on their progress in implementing their NDCs.
   - The GCF will provide support for monitoring and reporting activities, including the development of national monitoring systems.

5. **Financing**
   - The GCF will provide financing for climate change mitigation activities that are consistent with countries' NDCs.
   - This financing will be provided through grants and concessional loans.

6. **Technical assistance**
   - The GCF will provide technical assistance to countries to help them develop and implement their NDCs.
   - This assistance will include training, capacity-building, and other forms of technical support.

7. **Knowledge sharing**
   - The GCF will facilitate knowledge sharing among countries on best practices for climate change mitigation.
   - This will be done through workshops, conferences, and other forms of knowledge exchange.

8. **Stakeholder engagement**
   - The GCF will engage with stakeholders, including civil society organizations, the private sector, and other relevant actors, to ensure that the project is implemented in a transparent and inclusive manner.
   - Stakeholder engagement will be an ongoing process throughout the implementation of the project.

9. **Gender equality and social inclusion**
   - The GCF will promote gender equality and social inclusion in the implementation of the project.
   - This will include ensuring that women and marginalized groups are included in decision-making processes and have access to climate change mitigation activities.

10. **Environmental and social safeguards**
    - The GCF will ensure that environmental and social safeguards are in place to protect against negative impacts from climate change mitigation activities.
    - This will include measures to prevent and mitigate adverse effects on biodiversity, ecosystems, and human rights.

## E

### Implementation procedures for all components of the project will be based on the following principles:
- **Sustainable development** and climate resilience.
- **Participatory and active involvement** of local communities in planning, implementation, and monitoring.
- **Integrated approaches** to natural resource management with a focus on ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA).
- **Gender-sensitive** and culturally appropriate interventions.
- **Use of local materials** and traditional knowledge.
- **Capacity building** for local communities and government institutions.
- **Monitoring and evaluation** systems that track progress towards project objectives.

### Author of policy, procedures, and guidelines:
- **Policy:** Ministry of Environment and Climate Change (MECC).
- **Procedures:** National Climate Change Committee (NCCC).
- **Guidelines:** Local Government Units (LGUs).

### Progress reports on construction activities will be submitted to the GCF every six months.
- **Annual progress updates** will be submitted to the GCF every year.
- **Final evaluation report** will be submitted at the end of the project.

### ACRS will be responsible in GCF assessment and management procedures with the following:
- **Project Management Unit (PMU)** will be established to manage the project.
- **Annual work plans** will be developed and approved by the GCF.
- **Financial statements** will be prepared annually and submitted to the GCF.

### ARRA 10% local and national government contributions:
- **Local Government Units (LGUs)** will contribute 5% of the total project cost.
- **National Government Agencies (NGAs)** will contribute 5% of the total project cost.

### Project Management Consultant (PMC):
- **Consultant** will be engaged to provide technical assistance and support to the PMU.
- **Consultancy services** will be procured through a competitive bidding process.

### Direct costs:
- **Project management** (10% of total project cost).
- **Monitoring and evaluation** (5% of total project cost).

### Indirect costs:
- **Overhead costs** (7% of total project cost).

### Total direct costs:
- **PHP 62,707,000.00**.

### Total indirect costs:
- **PHP 43,900,000.00**.

### Total project cost:
- **PHP 105,607,000.00** (inclusive of 12% VAT).

### Based on the assumption that:
- **Exchange rate is PHP 50.00 per USD $1.00**.
- **Project will be implemented over a period of five years**.

### Note: Amounts are subject to methodology.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.1.1 PAGE 87

## E

- **Objective:** Ensure participation of non-Annex I parties in GCF activities.
  - Non-Annex I participants are also able to effectively learn from Annex I parties.

### Direct benefits
- Based on the assumption that 50% of the total budget is allocated for direct benefits, each of these 40,000 beneficiaries will receive $1,250.
  - This amount will enable them to purchase a new laptop and other necessary equipment.
  - Each of these households will also be able to afford a new refrigerator and a washing machine.

### Indirect benefits
- **Increased employment opportunities:** The program will create 40,000 new jobs in the technology sector.
  - This will help reduce unemployment rates among young people.
  - The program will also provide training for 20,000 women (75% female) to work as IT technicians.

### Shared benefits
- **Increased access to education:** The program will provide free laptops and internet access to 10,000 students in rural areas.
  - This will help improve their educational outcomes.
  - The program will also provide scholarships for 5,000 students (75% female) to pursue higher education.

### Long-term benefits
- **Increased economic growth:** The program will help create a more skilled workforce, which will lead to increased productivity and economic growth.
  - This will help reduce poverty rates in the long run.
  - The program will also help improve the country's international competitiveness by providing access to new technologies.

### Additional benefits
- **Improved health outcomes:** The program will provide free laptops and internet access to 10,000 patients in rural areas.
  - This will help improve their access to healthcare information and services.
  - The program will also provide scholarships for 5,000 students (75% female) to pursue medical education.

### Summary
- The Green Climate Fund funding proposal aims to provide direct, indirect, shared, long-term, and additional benefits to non-Annex I parties.
  - The program will help improve their access to technology, education, healthcare, and employment opportunities.
  - This will lead to increased economic growth, reduced poverty rates, and improved international competitiveness.

# E.6 Programmes activities and deliverables

## Objective 1.1

**Description:**
The EU will be involved in the development of climate resilience through the local implementation of climate change adaptation (CCA) practices and technologies.

**Sub-activities:**

### Sub-activity 1.1.1
Organize workshops on climate resilience at community level, with the participation of local authorities, NGOs, and other stakeholders to promote a shared vision for CCA.

### Sub-activity 1.1.2
Capture lessons on CCA from EU-funded projects and other sources to inform the development of local CCA plans.

### Sub-activity 1.1.3
Develop and implement climate risk assessments at community level, with the participation of local authorities, NGOs, and other stakeholders.

### Sub-activity 1.1.4
Promote the use of climate information services (CIS) to support decision-making on CCA.

**Deliverables:**

### Deliverable 1.1.1
Workshop reports from community-level workshops on climate resilience.

### Deliverable 1.1.2
Lessons learned and best practices from EU-funded projects and other sources.

### Deliverable 1.1.3
Climate risk assessments at the community level, with recommendations for CCA actions.

### Deliverable 1.1.4
Guidelines on the use of climate information services (CIS) to support decision-making on CCA.
