## Activity 1.4

Alongside 3000 farmers will be targeted to receive training on climate-smart agriculture (CSA) technologies and practices. This will include training on the use of new technologies such as solar-powered irrigation systems, weather-based crop insurance, and the use of climate information services under the FFP. The FFP will also be used to link these farmers with financial service providers to access credit for CSA investments.

The FFP will be used to link the farmers with financial service providers to access credit for CSA investments. The FFP will also be used to link the farmers with financial service providers to access credit for CSA investments. ## Activity 1.5 "Strengthening and scaling up climate-smart agriculture"

The activity focuses on strengthening the capacity of smallholder farmers to adopt climate-smart agriculture (CSA) practices. This includes training on CSA technologies, climate information services, and sustainable farming practices. The activity will also support the development of market linkages for CSA products.

Training activities will be conducted in collaboration with local NGOs and farmer organizations. The activity will also support the development of market linkages for CSA products.

## Activity 1.6 "Enhancing resilience to climate change"

The activity focuses on enhancing the capacity of smallholder farmers to adapt to climate change through the adoption of climate-smart agriculture (CSA) practices. This includes training on CSA technologies, climate information services, and sustainable farming practices. The activity will also support the development of market linkages for CSA products.

Training activities will be conducted in collaboration with local NGOs and farmer organizations. The activity will also support the development of market linkages for CSA products.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V1.0 | PAGE 8

## E

### 1.1. General principles and objectives

The general principles and objectives of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) are to:

- Promote a paradigm shift towards low-emission and climate-resilient development pathways by providing support to developing countries to enable them to limit or reduce their greenhouse gas emissions and adapt to the impacts of climate change.
- Support and promote a country-driven approach and transformative policies and action in developing countries.
- Encourage and maintain a balance of funding for mitigation and adaptation, taking into account country-driven priorities.

### 1.2.1. Country ownership

This means that the GCF has particular importance to the principles of country ownership and the principle of additionality. The GCF will support activities that are:

- **Country-led**: The GCF will support activities that are led by developing countries, based on their own priorities and needs.
- **Transformative**: The GCF will support activities that contribute to transformative change in developing countries.

### 1.2.2. Country systems

A well-functioning country system is essential for the effective implementation of GCF resources. The GCF will therefore:

- Support the development and strengthening of country systems, including national designated authorities (NDAs) and accredited entities.
- Encourage the use of country systems to access and manage GCF resources.

### 1.3.1. Private sector activities

The GCF will support private sector activities that are:

- **Country-driven**: The GCF will support private sector activities that are driven by the needs and priorities of developing countries.
- **Transformative**: The GCF will support private sector activities that contribute to transformative change in developing countries.

### 1.3.2. Value chain studies

The GCF will support value chain studies that:

- Identify opportunities for low-emission and climate-resilient development.
- Inform the design of GCF-funded projects and programs.

### 1.4.1. Results-based approaches

The GCF will use results-based approaches to ensure that its funding is used effectively and efficiently. The GCF will therefore:

- Develop and implement results-based approaches for all of its funding.
- Monitor and evaluate the results of its funding to ensure that it is achieving its objectives.

### 1.4.2. Gender equality

The GCF will promote gender equality in all of its activities. The GCF will therefore:

- Integrate gender equality into the design, implementation, and monitoring of all of its activities.
- Support activities that specifically target women and girls.

### 1.5.1. Annual report by each accredited entity on results and progress of implementation

Each accredited entity is required to submit an annual report to the GCF on the results and progress of implementation of its funded projects and programs.

## Output 1.3 Access to climate information and advisory services improved for climate responsive water use and long planning

### Agenda Item 1.3 Strengthening of the capacity of the project office

This activity and support is in alignment with the objectives of the project office, as outlined by the GoG/Phare SDGs implementation plan. It will be implemented through a combination of the following activities:

- **Sub-agency 1.3.1 Health outcomes for climate-resilient water use:** This sub-agency will focus on improving health outcomes related to climate-resilient water use.
- **Sub-agency 1.3.2.4 Leveraging additional financing:** This sub-agency will work with local and international partners to leverage additional financing for the project.

### Sub-agency 1.3.1 Health outcomes for climate-resilient water use

This sub-agency will focus on improving health outcomes related to climate-resilient water use. Specific activities include:

- **Sub-sub-agency 1.3.1.1 Health outcomes:** This sub-sub-agency will work to improve health outcomes by ensuring access to clean and safe water.
- **Sub-sub-agency 1.3.1.2 Water quality monitoring:** This sub-sub-agency will focus on monitoring the quality of water to ensure it meets health standards.

### Sub-agency 1.3.2.4 Leveraging additional financing

This sub-agency will work with local and international partners to leverage additional financing for the project. Specific activities include:

- **Sub-sub-agency 1.3.2.4.1 MOUs signed:** This sub-sub-agency will focus on signing Memorandums of Understanding (MOUs) with partners.
- **Sub-sub-agency 1.3.2.4.2 Financing proposals developed:** This sub-sub-agency will develop financing proposals to attract additional funding.

## Output 1.5 Evidence-based Policy Level Coordination and Advocacy

### Sub-agency 1.5.1 Evidence-based policy advocacy

This sub-agency will focus on evidence-based policy advocacy to ensure that the project is aligned with national policies and strategies. Specific activities include:

- **Sub-sub-agency 1.5.1.1 Policy alignment:** This sub-sub-agency will work to align the project with national policies.
- **Sub-sub-agency 1.5.1.2 Advocacy campaigns:** This sub-sub-agency will conduct advocacy campaigns to raise awareness about the project.

### Sub-agency 1.5.2.3 Evidence-based policy coordination

This sub-agency will focus on evidence-based policy coordination to ensure that the project is coordinated with other national and international initiatives. Specific activities include:

- **Sub-sub-agency 1.5.2.3.1 Policy alignment:** This sub-sub-agency will work to align the project with other national and international policies.
- **Sub-sub-agency 1.5.2.3.2 Coordination meetings:** This sub-sub-agency will conduct coordination meetings with relevant stakeholders.

### Sub-agency 1.5.4 Deliver results and outcomes

This sub-agency will focus on delivering the results and outcomes of the project. Specific activities include:

- **Sub-sub-agency 1.5.4.1 Results tracking:** This sub-sub-agency will track the results and outcomes of the project.
- **Sub-sub-agency 1.5.4.2 Outcomes reporting:** This sub-sub-agency will report on the outcomes of the project.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V.1.0 | PAGE 83

## Sub-Activity 1.3.1.1 Identify capacity needs in countries

The capacity development will focus on:
- Building national and local capacities to manage climate change risks.
- Strengthening the capacities of key stakeholders, including government agencies, private sector, civil society organizations, and communities to implement climate change adaptation planning and actions.

### Sub-Activity 1.3.1.2 Organize training workshops

Organizing training workshops for:
- National and local governments
- Private sector entities
- Civil society organizations
- Communities

### Sub-Activity 1.3.1.3 Develop training materials

Developing training materials to support the capacity development efforts.

## Sub-Activity 1.3.2 Organize studies

Organizing studies to:
- Assess climate change risks and vulnerabilities in countries.
- Identify adaptation options and strategies for reducing those risks and vulnerabilities.

### Sub-Activity 1.3.2.1 Conduct vulnerability assessments

Conducting vulnerability assessments to identify the most vulnerable sectors, communities, and populations.

### Sub-Activity 1.3.2.2 Develop adaptation plans

Developing adaptation plans based on the findings of the vulnerability assessments.

## Activity 4.3.3
### Improved Monitoring Plan Maps

The plan calls for improved plans to be developed based on the results of additional fieldwork and meetings with local communities. This will enable more accurate mapping of the landscape, including the identification of key areas that need to be protected or restored. The improved monitoring plan will also include a system for tracking changes in land use over time, which can help identify trends and inform future conservation efforts.

## Activity 4.3.1
### Level 2 Consultation

Level 2 consultation is required following the completion of the initial assessment. This includes engagement with local communities to gather their input on the proposed activities and to ensure that the project is aligned with their needs and priorities. The results of this consultation will be used to refine the project design and to develop a plan for implementing the activities.

## Activity 4.3.2
### Implementation Condition

Implementation condition works is to be completed by 1.8. This includes the preparation of the site, the procurement of materials, and the mobilization of resources needed to carry out the work. The implementation condition will also include a system for tracking progress and ensuring that the work is carried out in accordance with the approved plan.

## Activity 4.6
### Handling of the complaint

The complaint needs to be handled prior to the completion of the project. This includes an investigation into the allegations made by the complainant, as well as a review of the project's records and procedures. The results of this investigation will be used to determine whether any corrective action is needed and to ensure that the project is conducted in accordance with the approved plan.

The completed works is to handled over 1.4.1 prior to the completion of the project. This includes a final inspection of the work, as well as a review of the project's records and procedures. - **Objective 3.4 Water information system (ISWIM) established and operated in five pilot projects**

  Activity 2.4 Planning of information systems for water resources management and climate change adaptation

  - One of the main objectives is to promote integrated water resources management and climate change adaptation, including the development of an information system that addresses the needs of stakeholders.
  - This activity will be implemented through a series of workshops and training sessions with key stakeholders in the five pilot projects.
  - The ISWIM system will be designed to collect, store, and analyze data on water resources and climate change impacts. It will also provide tools for decision-making and early warning systems.

- **Activity 2.4 Design of an information system**

  - The design of the information system will include the development of a database that can store and manage data from various sources.
  - The system will be designed to be user-friendly and accessible to stakeholders, including government agencies, non-governmental organizations (NGOs), and community groups.
  - The system will also include tools for data visualization and reporting, as well as mechanisms for data sharing and collaboration.

- **Water information and ISWIM system**

## Output 2: Technical and capacity building support provided to NDCB at national and provincial levels

### Objective:
Strengthening of NDCB at national and provincial levels through technical assistance, training, and capacity building.

### Detailed Description (NDCB):
- **Objective:** Enhance the capacity of NDCB to effectively and efficiently implement the Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) and achieve long-term low greenhouse gas emission development.
- **Activities:**
  - Provide technical support for the development and implementation of NDCs.
  - Facilitate the integration of climate change considerations into national policies, strategies, and planning processes.
  - Enhance the capacity of NDCB to monitor, report, and verify (MRV) greenhouse gas emissions and adaptation actions.

### Output 2.1: Institutional strengthening and capacity building
- **Objective:** Strengthen institutional capacities at the national level for effective implementation of climate change mitigation and adaptation measures.
- **Activities:**
  - Develop and implement a comprehensive training program for NDCB staff.
  - Provide technical assistance to enhance the capacity of NDCB in policy development, planning, and monitoring.

### Output 2.2: Technical assistance for adaptation and mitigation
- **Objective:** Enhance the capacity of NDCB to provide technical assistance for climate change adaptation and mitigation activities.
- **Activities:**
  - Provide technical support for the development and implementation of climate change adaptation projects.
  - Facilitate access to international expertise and resources for climate change mitigation.

### Output 2.3: Training in technical capacities and tools
- **Objective:** Enhance the capacity of NDCB staff through training in technical capacities and tools required for effective implementation of NDCs.
- **Activities:**
  - Conduct training workshops on climate change adaptation and mitigation techniques.
  - Provide access to specialized tools and software for climate change modeling and analysis.

### Output 2.4: Capacity building for communication
- **Objective:** Enhance the capacity of NDCB staff in effective communication and outreach activities related to climate change.
- **Activities:**
  - Conduct training workshops on effective communication strategies.
  - Develop and implement a comprehensive communication strategy for NDCB.

### Output 2.5: Monitoring, reporting, and verification (MRV)
- **Objective:** Enhance the capacity of NDCB in monitoring, reporting, and verifying greenhouse gas emissions and adaptation actions.
- **Activities:**
  - Develop and implement a MRV system for greenhouse gas emissions.
  - Provide technical assistance to enhance the capacity of NDCB in data collection, analysis, and reporting.

### Output 2.6: Policy development and planning
- **Objective:** Enhance the capacity of NDCB in policy development and planning for climate change mitigation and adaptation.
- **Activities:**
  - Conduct training workshops on policy development and planning.
  - Provide technical assistance to enhance the capacity of NDCB in strategic planning and policy analysis.

### Output 2.7: Access to international expertise
- **Objective:** Enhance the capacity of NDCB through access to international expertise and resources for climate change mitigation and adaptation.
- **Activities:**
  - Facilitate partnerships with international organizations and experts.
  - Provide technical assistance to enhance the capacity of NDCB in accessing international funding and resources.

# E. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements

## 160 Reporting from MOWRAM to GGF

The programme-level monitoring and evaluation will be undertaken by MOWRAM in accordance with the requirements of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA). As the main implementing agency, MOWRAM will ensure that the programme-level monitoring and reporting systems are aligned and quality assured. The programme-level monitoring and reporting system will be designed to capture and analyse changes in the environment and social safeguards indicators. MOWRAM is also responsible for coordinating and preparing a consolidated report for GCF.

## 170 Monitoring and Evaluation

The monitoring and evaluation framework will be developed as an integral part of the Fund's programme, which will be aligned with the main banks for the monitoring and evaluation of the project. The monitoring and evaluation system will be designed to capture changes in the environment and social safeguards indicators. The monitoring and evaluation system will also include a gender-responsive approach to ensure that the programme is achieving its objectives.

## 175 Key Indicators

Project performance monitoring and evaluation will aim to ascertain the extent to which the project is achieving its intended results and outcomes through their respective indicators. The key performance indicators (KPIs) will be used to measure progress towards the project's objectives, including the following:

- **Environmental**: Changes in biodiversity, ecosystem services, and carbon stocks.
- **Social**: Changes in livelihoods, food security, and community resilience.
- **Economic**: Changes in agricultural productivity, market access, and income levels.

## 176 Sources of the key indicators for CAMA8

The key indicators will be derived from existing national systems, including those used by the Department of Environment and Natural Resources (DENR), the Department of Agriculture (DA), and other relevant agencies. The sources of the key indicators include:

- **Environmental**: Data on biodiversity, ecosystem services, and carbon stocks.

# F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F.1 Risk factors and mitigation measures (Page 3)

### Selected Risk Factor 1: Delay of Approval by GCF Board

- **Category:** Administrative
- **Probability:** Low
- **Impact:** Moderate

**Description:**
There is a risk related to the timing for submission of CABDAR by the GCF Board which is expected to be submitted in December 2023. The delay may affect the implementation schedule and the timeline of FAD and AIB and impact the financial returns from the project.

**Mitigation Measures:**
The NFA, FAD and AIB will try and expedite the process of design and approval to ensure that the RPP is submitted according to the agreed schedule and are responsive to changes at all levels.

### Selected Risk Factor 2: Technical & Operational Capacity

- **Category:** Technical & Operational
- **Probability:** Moderate
- **Impact:** High

**Description:**
The technical assessment report prepared by PPC and VIAPCOS in December 2022 noted that the work is an insufficient appraisal of human resources at all levels of the project management team. The lack of capacity to implement the project may lead to delays in the implementation of the project.

**Mitigation Measures:**
Based on the technical assessment report of the Water Resources Management Sector Committee, the project will be implemented by MOEW and the relevant institutions (i.e., MOEW, WRA, etc.). The project will also include a component for capacity building. The project will also include a component for capacity building.

### Selected Risk Factor 3: Policy and Regulatory Framework

- **Category:** Policy & Regulatory
- **Probability:** Moderate
- **Impact:** High

**Description:**
Based on the institutional assessment report of the Water Resources Management Sector Committee, the project will be implemented by MOEW and the relevant institutions (i.e., MOEW, WRA, etc.). The project will also include a component for capacity building. The project will also include a component for capacity building.

**Mitigation Measures:**
The project will be implemented in accordance with the policies, systems, procedures and guidelines of the Government of the Republic of the Union of Myanmar (GoRUM). The project will also include a component for capacity building.

## 160
**Description:** Enhance policies and strategies to promote climate change adaptation, which can mitigate impacts of natural disasters. Climate change has made impressive progress in innovation, technology change, strategies, and policies.

**Mitigation Measures:**
- 161: The policy offers incentives that the public sector is aware of climate risks and the importance of managing climate impacts. It is important to ensure that the public sector is aware of the risks and opportunities associated with climate change.
- 162: Lack of coordination at the municipal level is a common issue in the country and also impacts the ability to implement climate change adaptation measures at the local and sub-national levels. This could be driven by limited resources or lack of capacity.

**Mitigation Measures:**
- 163: A robust coordination mechanism has been improved establishing a Project Steering Committee, which will ensure that all stakeholders are involved in the process. The project will also include the development of a monitoring and evaluation framework to support the implementation of the project.
- 164: The project will be implemented through the National Climate Change Adaptation Fund (NCCAF) and the National Climate Change Mitigation Fund (NCCMF). These funds will be used to support the implementation of the project.

**Selected Risk Factors:** A. Risk communication among stakeholders

## 165
**Description:** Lack of coordination at the municipal level is a common issue in the country and also impacts the ability to implement climate change adaptation measures at the local and sub-national levels. This could be driven by limited resources or lack of capacity.

**Mitigation Measures:**
- 166: A robust coordination mechanism has been improved establishing a Project Steering Committee, which will ensure that all stakeholders are involved in the process. The project will also include the development of a monitoring and evaluation framework to support the implementation of the project.
- 167: The project will be implemented through the National Climate Change Adaptation Fund (NCCAF) and the National Climate Change Mitigation Fund (NCCMF). These funds will be used to support the implementation of the project.

**Selected Risk Factors:** B. Inadequate technical capacity

## 168
**Description:** A simplified process for the approval of international programs promotes or funds large contracts for climate change adaptation and mitigation projects.

**Mitigation Measures:**
- 169: CAFFIL will establish a dedicated team of national and international procurement experts to conduct, implement, and approve as efficiently as possible. IPAD will lead and support this effort.

# GREAT CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V.1.1 PAGE 9

## Selected Risk Factor 6: Social and Environmental

### Category | Probability | Impact
- | - | -
**Other** | **Low** | **Description**

#### EBI
Social social and environmental risks have been identified in the ESMP (Table 5). These risks can negatively impact the project environment and the livelihoods of local population, especially those who depend on natural resources for their livelihood. The impacts can be exacerbated by poor planning, inadequate or ineffective management, and lack of community engagement. The major potential adverse environmental impacts include deforestation, loss of biodiversity, soil erosion, and water pollution.

#### Mitigation Measures
- A SECAP mission was deployed to identify the potential social and environmental risks prior to project design which identified the following and proposed that it would be addressed in case of approval:
  - Deforestation: The project will be designed to avoid deforestation.
  - Biodiversity: The project will be designed to avoid biodiversity loss.
  - Soil erosion: The project will be designed to avoid soil erosion.
  - Water pollution: The project will be designed to avoid water pollution.

The mitigation measures include:
- Community engagement and awareness raising on environmental issues, including climate change adaptation and mitigation.
- Environmental impact assessment (EIA) and social impact assessment (SIA).
- Environmental management plan (EMP), including biodiversity management, soil conservation, and water resource management.
- Regular monitoring of the project implementation to ensure compliance with the EMP.

### Selected Risk Factor 7: Public Sector Engagement

#### Category | Probability | Impact
- | - | -
**Other** | **Medium** | **Description**

The public sector engagement is critical for the success of the project. The project requires strong support from the government and other stakeholders to ensure effective implementation and sustainability.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal V.1.0 - PAGE F

## F

### 188
The private sector has included indicative fees with supplier farmers due to the fragmented nature of their production and limited demand from their inputs.

### Mitigation Measures

#### 189
**CAFIAR** proposes to enhance capacity building for smallholder farmers to systematically engage the private sector for input supply and access to markets. In general, this will include training on financial management and business planning skills in doing business in the agricultural sector.

##### Selected Risk Factor: Risk of monetary lending, internal financing or prohibited practices

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Oblig** | **Medium** | **Medium** |

###### Description
IFAD included financial management and procurement arrangements during the project design phase. The project will include a review of financial management and procurement arrangements that meet IFAD's standards. However, it is not expected to include any new financial management or procurement actions.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

#### 190
Proposed mitigation measures include:
- Include IFAD's template of self-certification forms for all suppliers.
- Develop a standard operating procedure (SOP) for the review and approval of supplier invoices, including the verification of IFAD's standards.
- Include a clause in the procurement contract requiring suppliers to comply with IFAD's standards.

#### 191
Proposed mitigation measures include:
- Include IFAD's template of self-certification forms for all suppliers.
- Develop a standard operating procedure (SOP) for the review and approval of supplier invoices, including the verification of IFAD's standards.
- Include a clause in the procurement contract requiring suppliers to comply with IFAD's standards.

#### 192
Under IFAD's Affirmative Action Loan (AAL) Credit Facility (ACF), the financing of women-led small and medium enterprises (SMEs) is included. The financing of AAL SMEs is conditional on compliance with IFAD's gender equality policies, including the requirement to provide a minimum of 50% of the loan amount to women-owned or managed businesses.

# GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## S1. Environmental and Social Assessment (ESA)

### S1.79. Works, expenditures

The proposed environmental and social risk management system will be based on the following principles:

- Risk Category Assignment and Rationale: The proposed environmental and social risk management system will include a risk assessment of EAS risks and impacts that may arise from proposed projects. It is noted that risk assessment takes into account the following:
  - Quality, scope, and scale of project activities.
  - Potential adverse environmental and social impacts.
  - Sensitivity of potential project areas to environmental and social impacts.
  - Vulnerability of biodiversity resources in the project's area of influence.
  - Sensitivity of indigenous peoples' lands and territories.

Risk management becomes the foundation for risk mitigation, and the capacity of affected communities to manage risks. The EAS risk management measures should be tailored to the specific needs of each individual project.

### S1.80. Main Outcomes, ESCM, and Mitigation Measures

To ensure that the environmental and social impacts of EAS are minimized and managed effectively, the following outcomes have been identified:
- Compliance with the ESA requirements.
- Climate Resilient Project Design.
- Climate Resilient Project Implementation.

The main outcomes of the ESA process include:
- Identification of potential adverse environmental and social impacts.
- Assessment of risks associated with these impacts.
- Development of mitigation measures to address these risks.
- Monitoring and evaluation of the effectiveness of these measures.

The EAS instruments highlight the importance of integrating climate resilience into project design, implementation, and monitoring. Environmental risks and impacts were considered in the development of the ESA requirements. The EAS process includes:
- Interlinked links, environmental risk management, and climate resilience.
- Development of mitigation measures to address these risks.
- Monitoring and evaluation of the effectiveness of these measures.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V.1.0 | PAGE 18

## 200. Stakeholder Consultations
Public consultations occurred at various project stages, including baseline studies and during preparation of E&As, involving various methods of stakeholder engagement (public well and environmental health clinics). Climate Management Plans (CMPs) were developed to address potential impacts on local communities.

## 201. Safeguard Register
A safeguards register was prepared in accordance with the GCF’s Environmental and Social Policy. This register identifies potential adverse impacts that may include risks to biodiversity, cultural heritage, and indigenous peoples. It also includes measures to mitigate these impacts.

## 202. Indigenous Peoples
An Indigenous Peoples Planning Framework is in place, with consultation and engagement activities conducted throughout the project cycle. Indigenous Peoples are not present in the project’s area of influence; however, their sub-national governments have expressed a willingness to be consulted when detailed engineering designs are finalized.

### G.1 Gender assessment and action plan (308 words, approximately 2 pages)
A gender assessment has been conducted in accordance with the principles and requirements of GCF’s Gender Policy. The assessment examines the status quo for women and men, including their needs, capacities, and vulnerabilities. It also provides recommendations to promote equality between genders.

### G.2 Key findings of the Gender Assessment
The key findings indicate that in the project area:
- Women are more likely to be engaged in agriculture (70%).
- Men are more likely to be involved in non-agricultural activities.
- Women have less access to education and training opportunities.
- Women face higher levels of poverty and lower income.

### G.3 Gender mainstreaming
Gender mainstreaming has been integrated into the project design, including gender-responsive procurement processes and equal opportunity policies for both men and women.

## 205. Addressing gender issues in environmental and social impact assessments (ESIA)
Gender issues are addressed in the ESIA process to ensure that potential impacts on women and men are considered equally.

## 5.1 Financial management and procurement plans: 90 words, approximately 1 page

### 207. Procurement Arrangements
The procurement arrangements for the project will be aligned with the CARE/IRC project procurement manual. The procurement process will follow the standard procurement procedures as outlined in the Public Expenditure and Financial Accountability (PEFA) framework. The procurement process will include the following steps: (i) identification of needs, (ii) preparation of procurement plans, (iii) competitive bidding, (iv) evaluation of bids, (v) award of contract, (vi) procurement of goods and services, (vii) inspection and acceptance of goods and services, and (viii) payment.

### 208. Financial Management
The financial management for the project will be conducted according to the IFAO Procurement Manual. This analysis shows that the current financial management is not adequate to meet the needs of the project. The financial management arrangements include: (i) budgeting, (ii) accounting, (iii) procurement, (iv) internal controls, and (v) reporting. The financial management system will be designed to ensure transparency and accountability in the use of funds.

### 209. Information Management
The information management for the project will be conducted according to the IFAO Procurement Manual. This analysis shows that the current information management is not adequate to meet the needs of the project. The information management arrangements include: (i) data collection, (ii) data storage and retrieval, (iii) data analysis and interpretation, (iv) reporting, and (v) dissemination of information.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal

## External audits

### CAIBAR (project financial management)

The audit will be conducted by a private firm selected through an open and transparent process. The audit will be conducted in accordance with Caribbean Auditing Standards, which is aligned with International Standards on Auditing (ISA). The audit will assess the adequacy of internal controls and procedures for financial reporting and compliance with GCF policies and procedures. The audit will also include an assessment of the project's internal control environment.

### IADB (project management)

There will be a single auditor, appointed External Auditor (EAO) under the MOC, represented by the IEE in the Subsidiary Agreements (SA) with the Executing Entity. The EAO will be responsible for conducting the audit and reporting to the GCF. The budget for the audit will be based on the cost of the audit and will include the costs of the audit team, travel, and other related expenses. The audit will be conducted in accordance with the IADB's internal control standards.

**Subject: No-objection to the project titled CADAIR in Cambodia as included in the funding proposal submitted by the Government of Cambodia (GOC) for GEF/FAO/National Climate Adaptive Irrigation and Sustainable Agriculture Project (CADAIR)**

Dear Mr. Quaet,

We refer to the project titled CADAIR in Cambodia as included in the funding proposal submitted by the Government of Cambodia (GOC) for GEF/FAO National Climate Adaptive Irrigation and Sustainable Agriculture Project (CADAIR). We have reviewed the project and we acknowledge that the project will contribute to the authorized representative of the Ministry of Environment, the national designated authority, has no objection to the implementation of this project. We hereby communicate our no-objection to the project by confirming our no-objection. It is mentioned that:

- The project will be implemented in the funding period: 2004-2008.
- The project will be implemented in conformity with the national priorities, strategies and plans of Cambodia.
- The project will contribute to the achievement of the GEF/FAO strategic goals.
- The project is included in the funding proposal in conformity with relevant procedures.

We also confirm that our national process for accepting no-objection to the project has been followed. We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GEF website. Please, Mr. Quaet, accept the assurances of my consideration and regards.

Yours sincerely,

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP270

## Proposal name
Climate Adaptive Irrigation and Sustainable Agriculture for Food Security in Cambodia

## Accredited entity
International Fund for Agricultural Development

## Country
Cambodia

## Project size
Medium

## L. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

### Strengths
- The project targets high-risk provinces with a high proportion of poor smallholder farmers.
- It focuses on water management and water use efficiency, where agricultural households are highly vulnerable to climate change.
- Water is a vital input for promoting opportunities in agriculture.
- The project aligns well with the country’s climate change adaptation strategy.
- The project effectively combines adaptation and mitigation objectives, targeting sub-national levels of high vulnerability density.

### Points of concern
- The proposed directly omits adaptation benefits by setting planning horizons with a 10-year budget.
- The project is not aligned with the country’s National Adaptation Plan (NAP) process.
- The project does not align well with the country’s climate change policy and strategy.
- The project does not effectively integrate adaptation and mitigation objectives.

### Recommendations
- The project should be integrated into the International Fund for Agricultural Development’s (IFAD) work on climate change adaptation and mitigation.
- The project should be aligned with the country’s National Adaptation Plan (NAP) process.
- The project should align well with the country’s climate change policy and strategy.
- The project should effectively integrate adaptation and mitigation objectives.

### Additional comments
- With clear focus on opportunities and challenges, the project could benefit from a longer planning horizon of at least 20 years, which would allow for more effective long-term actions.

# I.

The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal in accordance with the terms stated in the terms sheet signed between the Secretariat and the accredited entity (AE), Green Climate Fund (GCF) for the project titled "Climate Adaptation Irrigation and Sustainable Agriculture for Redd+ Beneficiaries" (CASAB) with GCF reference number GCF/RA/202.

# II. Summary of the Secretariat's assessment

## 2.1 Project background

The objective of the Climate Adaptation Irrigation and Sustainable Agriculture for Redd+ (CASAB) project is to strengthen the resilience of Cambodia’s rural communities and ecosystems through improved water resource management, sustainable agriculture practices, and climate-resilient livelihoods. The project aims to enhance the adaptive capacity of vulnerable communities in Preah Vihear, Stung Treng, Kratie, and Kampong Cham provinces by promoting integrated water resource management, agricultural practices, and climate-smart technologies. The project seeks to address rising temperatures, erratic rainfall patterns, and increased frequency of droughts and floods, which are increasingly affecting the high-population density provinces of Preah Vihear, Stung Treng, Kratie, and Kampong Cham.

The project will be implemented in three phases over a period of ten years. The first component focuses on enhancing water resource management by developing small-scale irrigation systems, rainwater harvesting, and soil moisture conservation techniques. The second component aims to strengthen the enabling environment for climate-resilient agriculture through capacity building, technology transfer, and policy advocacy. The third component involves the scaling up of successful practices from the first two components.

The project will be implemented in collaboration with key stakeholders including the Ministry of Water Resources and Meteorology (MOWRAM), the Royal University of Agriculture (RUA), and other relevant government agencies. The project will also engage local communities, non-governmental organizations, and private sector entities to ensure effective implementation and sustainability.

CASAB is aligned with the Government of Cambodia’s National Adaptation Plan (NAP) process and contributes to the achievement of the country's Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs). The project supports the government's efforts to enhance climate resilience and sustainable development, in line with the Paris Agreement and other international commitments.

CASAB is funded directly through GCF/RA/202 grant proposal and reaches an additional 1,153,189 people indirectly benefiting from cross-sectoral engagement. The project also contributes to a range of Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), including SDG 6 on clean water and sanitation, SDG 11 on sustainable cities and communities, SDG 13 on climate action, and SDG 2 on zero hunger.

The total project cost is USD 80 million (USD 40 million grant and USD 40 million loan), with contributions from GCF funding USD 80 million (USD 40 million grant and USD 40 million loan).

## Component-by-component analysis

### Component 1: Field-level climate adaptation and resilience (total cost: USD 8 million; GEF and IDA 20.23 million)

Component 1 focuses on strengthening local climate adaptation and resilience by engaging smallholder farmers with the knowledge, tools, and support necessary to adapt to changing weather patterns. This includes promoting improved crop varieties, soil and water management techniques, and integrated pest management (IPM) practices. The project will also support the development of climate-smart agriculture and sustainable farming systems through farmer field schools, capacity building, and


---
**Page 123**
---

# Green Climate Fund

## GCF-044/13/PAB/12

Demonstration lots, farmers will gain hands-on experience in recent farming methods relevant to local conditions. Special emphasis is placed on conserving water, soil and biodiversity. The component also supports the development of community-based organizations and local institutions.

The project operates in a highly climate-vulnerable environment where extreme weather events are frequent. Climate change impacts include increased frequency and intensity of droughts, floods, storms and cyclones. These events result in crop failure, irrigation infrastructure and agricultural equipment damage, livestock mortality and displacement of people. The project will address this issue by promoting climate-smart agriculture practices that improve soil fertility, water use efficiency and resilience to extreme weather events. It will also support the development of community-based early warning systems for extreme weather events.

### Component 1: Strengthening and climate-proofing water sub-sectors (for demonstration climate-resilient pilot test; GCF-044/13/PAB/12; USD 6.7 million)

The project will support the Government of Cambodia to strengthen its water resources management systems and improve local productivity in the face of climate change. The project will focus on improving water resource management, including canals, water storage facilities and control structures, which are critical for agricultural production. It will also promote integrated water resource management at the watershed level. The project will also strengthen the capacity of WASH (Water, Sanitation and Hygiene) sub-sectors to improve climate resilience.

The project will include activities such as:

- Improving water storage facilities for agricultural input and output.
- Building local capacity for climate-smart agriculture.
- Promoting integrated water resource management at the watershed level.

### Component 2: Strengthening institutional and regulatory capacity for low-emission climate-resilient development (GCF-044/13/PAB/12; USD 6.7 million)

The project will support the Government of Cambodia to strengthen its institutional and regulatory capacity for low-emission climate-resilient development. The project will focus on improving the capacity of government agencies to develop and implement policies and regulations that promote climate resilience.

The project will include activities such as:

- Developing and implementing national policies and strategies for climate change adaptation.
- Strengthening the capacity of government agencies to develop and implement policies and regulations that promote climate resilience.

# Green Climate Fund

## I. Project support for climate innovation and sustainable activities

The project supports a range of innovative and sustainable activities designed to strengthen environmental capacity and climate resilience for resilient water and land management systems. The project will be implemented through a combination of direct interventions and adaptation approaches that include national and sub-national government agencies, private sector actors, and local communities. The project will enhance the development of climate-smart agriculture, improve water resource management, support capacity building, and promote sustainable practices in farming. Capacity development is also a core component, with strong provision by key stakeholders to ensure effective implementation and monitoring. The project will be implemented through a combination of direct investment and capacity-building activities, including MOUs with the Ministry of Environment and Forests (MoEF), and the Department of Agriculture and Cooperation (DAC). The project will support the development of climate-smart agriculture practices at the regional and local level and design a decision-making framework for sustainable land management. The project will also support the establishment of a Central Project Implementation Unit with strong technical expertise and capacity to manage and implement the project.

### General objectives

- The project, classified as category A, recognizes significant positive environmental and social impacts.
- The project is designed to be consistent with the Green Climate Fund's (GCF) environmental and social safeguards.
- The project will respond to the need for capable management of a potential adverse impact, identified through an Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA).
- The project will work closely with BCF and the MoEF authorities, and supported by international school standards to ensure effective implementation.

### Project Management

The project will be managed by the PMI, supported by technical specialists in areas like agriculture, water management, and climate change. The PMI includes experienced staff from relevant ministries and departments.

## II. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

### 2.1 Impact potential

The project is considered to have significant impact potential, aiming to transform the vulnerability landscape of Colombia's rural agricultural investments through targeted interventions. The project will support the development of climate-smart agriculture practices in Colombia, which can be replicated and scaled up.

#### Scale: High

- The project will directly benefit 34,257 people (22,163 female) and indirectly reach an additional 80,000 people.
- The project will contribute to the achievement of the GCF's objectives for climate change adaptation and mitigation.


---
**Page 125**
---

# Green Climate Fund

## 3.2 Paradigm shift potential

### a.
The project aims to accelerate a significant shift towards climate resilience and sustainable development by promoting integrated approaches that address multiple climate risks simultaneously. This requires a paradigm shift in how climate risks are perceived to move away from single-sector adaptation planning towards integrated, agile planning positions that encompass the full range of climate risks and their interactions with social, economic, environmental, and institutional factors. This includes integrated water resource management, sustainable land use, ecosystem-based adaptation, and disaster risk reduction.

To achieve this, the project promotes comprehensive approaches combining climate-smart agriculture, sustainable forestry, and integrated water resource management. It builds on an adaptive approach to climate change that integrates multiple sectors and stakeholders in a participatory manner. This includes promoting climate-resilient farming systems, improving agricultural productivity and sustainability, and enhancing ecosystem resilience.

### b.
The project implements a range of climate-smart interventions such as AWD, SDMR, CA, and agroforestry. It also enhances the enabling environment by providing information and early warnings to farmers through improved weather forecasting and climate services. This includes promoting integrated approaches that combine climate-smart agriculture with sustainable land management practices, such as agroforestry, conservation agriculture, and water-efficient irrigation systems.

### c.
The project aims to enhance the resilience of 150,000 smallholder farmers in Cambodia by implementing climate-smart agriculture interventions. This includes promoting integrated approaches that combine climate-smart agriculture with sustainable land management practices, such as agroforestry, conservation agriculture, and water-efficient irrigation systems.

## 3.3 Sustainable development potential

### a.
The project is strategically poised to contribute significantly to sustainable development outcomes by enhancing the resilience of vulnerable communities and ecosystems. It aims to achieve multiple Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), including SDG 1 (No Poverty), SDG 2 (Zero Hunger), SDG 6 (Clean Water and Sanitation), SDG 7 (Affordable and Clean Energy), SDG 8 (Decent Work and Economic Growth), SDG 9 (Industry, Innovation, and Infrastructure), SDG 13 (Climate Action), and SDG 15 (Life on Land).

### b.
By adopting integrated approaches, the project addresses multiple climate risks simultaneously and enhances the resilience of vulnerable communities. It contributes to sustainable development outcomes by promoting climate-smart agriculture, improving water resource efficiency, reducing greenhouse gas emissions, strengthening Climate Adaptation Opportunities, and enhancing ecosystem services. The project also promotes sustainable land management practices, including agroforestry, conservation agriculture, and integrated water resource management.

### c.
The project aims to enhance the resilience of 150,000 smallholder farmers in Cambodia by implementing climate-smart agriculture interventions. This includes promoting integrated approaches that combine climate-smart agriculture with sustainable land management practices, such as agroforestry, conservation agriculture, and water-efficient irrigation systems.

# Green Climate Fund

## Needs of the recipient

### Scale: High

# Green Climate Fund

## Farm-level education and irrigation infrastructure investments are economically viable, with economic internal rates of return ranging from 13.8 to 27.1 percent across the six countries. The benefits of these investments exceed the costs by a factor of two to five times, including direct income increases or household reductions of up to $9 per capita.

### Key findings
- **Economic viability**: Farm-level investments in education and irrigation infrastructure are economically viable.
- **High internal rates of return**: These investments have high internal rates of return (13.8 to 27.1 percent).
- **Benefits exceed costs**: The benefits of these investments exceed the costs by a factor of two to five times.

### GCF perspective
The Green Climate Fund (GCF) can support these investments through its private sector window, which provides grants targeting non-recourse financing and co-financing. This approach aligns with the GCF’s overall strategy to leverage private sector resources for climate change mitigation and adaptation. The GCF can also reduce the great risks associated with these investments by providing guarantees or first-loss cover.

The ESCMF is an integrated environmental and social management framework. The management rights between the project's companies with ISCF's EIMS include environmental management, social impact assessment, and other related issues. An important part of the ESCMF is the Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA) process, which includes the following:

- **Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA)**: This involves the assessment and management of environmental and social impacts and risks associated with the project. It includes the identification, evaluation, and mitigation of potential adverse effects on the environment and communities.

- **Social Impact Assessment (SIA)**: This is a process to identify and evaluate the social consequences of a development project. It aims to ensure that the project benefits both the local community and the wider society by addressing issues such as resettlement, employment, health, and cultural impacts.

- **Environmental Management System (EMS)**: The EMS is an internationally recognized standard for managing environmental aspects and impacts of organizations. It helps companies to improve their environmental performance through a systematic approach.

The ISCF's EIMS includes the following:

- **ESCMF**: This framework integrates environmental, social, and economic considerations into the project's management processes.

# Learning and working conditions

The learning and working conditions standards ensure that all workers have safe and healthy working environments, fair wages, and equal opportunities for career advancement. The standards also promote respect for human rights and cultural diversity in the workplace. The ILO's core labor standards are:

- **Freedom of association and collective bargaining**: Workers should be free to join trade unions and bargain collectively.
- **Elimination of forced labor**: No one should be forced to work against their will under threat or punishment.
- **Abolition of child labor**: Children should not be employed in hazardous work that is detrimental to their health, safety, or morals.
- **Elimination of discrimination in respect of employment and occupation**: Everyone should have equal opportunities for employment without discrimination based on race, color, sex, religion, political opinion, national extraction, social origin, or other status.

The ILO's core labor standards are internationally recognized as the minimum requirements for decent work. They provide a framework for governments, employers, and workers' organizations to promote and protect workers' rights. The standards also serve as a basis for international cooperation and assistance in promoting decent work.

# ILO's core labor standards

The ILO's core labor standards include:

- **Freedom of association and collective bargaining**: Workers have the right to form and join trade unions and bargain collectively.
- **Elimination of forced labor**: No one should be subjected to forced or compulsory labor.
- **Abolition of child labor**: Children under a certain age should not be employed in hazardous work.
- **Elimination of discrimination in respect of employment and occupation**: Everyone should have equal opportunities for employment without discrimination.

These standards are internationally recognized as the minimum requirements for decent work. They provide a framework for governments, employers, and workers' organizations to promote and protect workers' rights. The standards also serve as a basis for international cooperation and assistance in promoting decent work.

# Green Climate Fund

## GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy & ESG (Environmental and Social Governance) Policies

The GCF has committed to ensuring that its operations are consistent with the principles of environmental and social governance. Environmental and social safeguards policies are included in the ESGF, which contains the following key elements:

- **Environmental and Social Management System**: This system ensures that all projects financed by the GCF adhere to environmental and social standards.
- **Environmental and Social Impact Assessments (ESIA)**: These assessments ensure that projects do not have negative impacts on the environment or local communities.
- **Indigenous Peoples Policy**: This policy aims to protect the rights of indigenous peoples and ensure their participation in decision-making processes related to GCF-funded activities.

### Implementation Arrangements

The implementation of this policy will be guided by the following principles:

1. **Free, Prior, Informed Consent (FPIC)**: The consent of indigenous peoples must be obtained before any project affecting them is undertaken.
2. **Participation**: Indigenous Peoples must participate in all stages of project planning and implementation.
3. **Non-Discrimination**: Projects must not discriminate against any group based on their ethnicity or cultural background.
4. **Respect for Human Rights**: All projects must respect the human rights of indigenous peoples, including their right to self-determination.

### ESGF Cultural Heritage

The greatest challenge potentially lies in impacts on cultural heritage and sacred sites. The GCF will work with its stakeholders to ensure that these impacts are minimized by:

- Conducting thorough assessments of cultural heritage sites.
- Incorporating the protection of cultural heritage into project design and implementation plans.
- Ensuring that indigenous peoples have a say in the management of their cultural heritage.

### Implementation Arrangements

The implementation of the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy will be guided by the following principles:

1. **Free, Prior, Informed Consent (FPIC)**: The consent of indigenous peoples must be obtained before any project affecting them is undertaken.
2. **Participation**: Indigenous Peoples must participate in all stages of project planning and implementation.
3. **Non-Discrimination**: Projects must not discriminate against any group based on their ethnicity or cultural background.
4. **Respect for Human Rights**: All projects must respect the human rights of indigenous peoples, including their right to self-determination.

The GCF will work with its stakeholders to ensure that these impacts are minimized by:

- Conducting thorough assessments of cultural heritage sites.
- Incorporating the protection of cultural heritage into project design and implementation plans.
- Ensuring that indigenous peoples have a say in the management of their cultural heritage.

# IFAD will be the AE for this project

Since the late 1990s, IFAD has been implementing 12 projects with a total project cost of US$ 1 billion in agricultural and rural development interventions in the country. The projects are financed by IFAD, the Government of Cambodia (GoC), and the NCDO component.

## G-G Project-specific evaluation risks (medium risk)

### c) Credit Risk
The borrower of the G-G loan will be the Government of Cambodia (GoC). The GoC is classified as a low credit risk borrower. However, the project will face several challenges given the importance and breadth of policies, measures, that need to be implemented in order to achieve the project objectives. The GoC has signed an IPSA with other co-financiers such as IFAD and NCDO. In case of delay or default by any co-financier, the GoC will be responsible for the repayment of the loan. The GoC has a strong commitment to the project and is properly managing its public finances in case of consolidation or delay in the co-financing.

### d) Compliance Risk
The project is being implemented in alignment with developing regulatory frameworks. The project will be implemented through a decentralized approach, which requires strong coordination among various stakeholders at different levels. The project will be implemented by the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries (MAFF). This is a government agency that has a good track record of implementing projects funded by IFAD.

IFAD has established control measures to mitigate these risks, including a thorough due diligence process, regular monitoring, and supervision of the project implementation. The project also benefits from strong partnerships with MAFF/CUCF representatives. The project also benefits from strong partnerships with MAFF/CUCF representatives. The project will be implemented through a decentralized approach, which requires strong coordination among various stakeholders at different levels.

While such risks exist, they are mitigated by the strong commitment of the GoC to the project and its importance in addressing the challenges facing the agricultural sector. Considering these factors, the overall compliance risk being for the project is medium.

### e) GO Portfolio Management Risk
In the case of a delay, the impact of this proposal on the G-G portfolio risk remains low. The proposed project will be implemented under the same proposal of all concentrations.

## Recommendation

It is recommended that the Board consider the above factors in its decision.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP270

**Proposal name:** Climate Adaptive Irrigation and Sustainable Agriculture for Resilience (CASAR) in Cambodia

**Accredited entity:** International Fund for Agricultural Development

**Country:** Cambodia

**Project size:** Medium

## I. Assessment of the Independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Overview

1. **Prevent vulnerability of Cambodia’s agriculture sector.** As a consequence of poor infrastructure and limited capacity, Cambodia’s agricultural sector is highly vulnerable to climate change. This includes exposure to flooding and reduced water availability during the dry season. The CASAR project aims to enhance the resilience of smallholder farmers in target provinces by improving irrigation systems and increasing access to weather information services.

2. **Climate change as a threat multiplier.** The impacts of climate change are exacerbating existing vulnerabilities, including poverty, food insecurity, and limited access to essential services. Climate change is expected to increase the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as floods, droughts, and heatwaves. These climate shocks disproportionately affect vulnerable populations, including smallholder farmers who rely on rainfed agriculture for their livelihoods.

3. **Climate resilience as a priority.** The CASAR project is designed to strengthen Cambodia’s agricultural resilience, with a specific focus on improving the capacity of smallholder farmers to adapt to climate change. This includes enhancing irrigation systems and early warning mechanisms, as well as providing training and support services to help farmers build their resilience.

4. **Climate adaptation as a key priority.** The CASAR project is aligned with the Government of Cambodia’s National Strategic Development Plan (NSDP) for 2019-2023, which aims to enhance climate resilience and reduce poverty. The project also supports the government’s efforts to achieve the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), particularly SDG 1 (no poverty), SDG 6 (clean water and sanitation), SDG 7 (affordable and clean energy), SDG 9 (industry, innovation, and infrastructure), and SDG 13 (climate action).

5. **Enhanced environmental governance.** Far-reaching climate adaptation efforts to enable sustainable development and poverty reduction are essential for achieving the CASAR project’s objectives.

# Green Climate Fund

## Project Title: CASBAR

### Project Description:
The project aims to integrate climate-smart agriculture (CSA) practices and technologies into existing farming systems and value chains in Ethiopia, Uganda, and Tanzania. The integration of CSA will enhance resilience to climate change and improve food security for smallholder farmers.

#### Key Features:
- **Total Funding Package:** USD 28.1 million (GCF funding: USD 20.3 million; Co-financing: USD 7.8 million)
- **Implementing Entities:**
  - World Agroforestry Centre (ICRAF) as the main implementing entity
  - National Agricultural Development Programme (NADP) in Uganda and Tanzania
  - National Meteorological Agency (NMA) in Ethiopia

#### Project Objectives:
1. Integrate CSA practices into existing farming systems.
2. Enhance climate information services to support farmers' decision-making.
3. Strengthen the capacity of national institutions to manage climate risks.

### Impact Potential:

#### Adaptation Impact:
The CASBAR project aims at integrating the climate resilience measures and technologies into existing farming systems and value chains in Ethiopia, Uganda, and Tanzania. By rehabilitating and restoring degraded landscapes, promoting sustainable land management practices, and enhancing the productivity of agricultural lands, the project will contribute to food security and improve livelihoods.

#### Mitigation Impact:
The CASBAR project aims at reducing greenhouse gas emissions from agriculture by promoting climate-smart agriculture (CSA) practices. The project will support the adoption of CSA practices that enhance carbon sequestration in soils and reduce emissions from agricultural activities.

### Scale: Low to medium

# Greenpeace Report

## Summary of Findings

Greenpeace has identified significant environmental and social impacts from direct-to-solar-powered irrigation systems, which have led to improved livelihoods and resource management. Such projects aim to reduce carbon emissions and stimulate local economies by replacing diesel-powered pumps with solar energy. However, these projects also pose risks such as water scarcity, land degradation, and increased greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions due to the need for additional water pumping.

### Key Findings

- **Water Scarcity**: Solar irrigation systems can exacerbate existing water scarcity issues.
- **Land Degradation**: Increased water use can lead to soil salinization and land degradation.
- **GHG Emissions**: Additional water pumping can increase GHG emissions, offsetting the benefits of solar power.

### Risks to Ignore

The rehabilitation of a 100 km road may lead to increased gas (GHG) emissions. The Green Climate Fund (GCF) is funding this project, which aims to reduce environmental and climate impact.

- **Asphalting**: Asphalt is a significant source of GHG emissions.
- **Deforestation**: Deforestation can occur due to the need for additional materials.
- **Social Impacts**: Local communities may face displacement or other social impacts.

### Recommendations

1. **Deforestation Risks**: The GCF should consider the risks associated with deforestation and ensure that projects do not contribute to further environmental degradation.
2. **GHG Emissions**: Projects should be designed to minimize GHG emissions from additional water pumping.
3. **Social Impacts**: The GCF should address social impacts, including potential displacement of local communities.

### Conclusion

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) must carefully consider the environmental and social impacts of its projects. This report highlights the need for comprehensive risk assessments and the integration of sustainable practices to ensure that climate change mitigation efforts do not inadvertently cause further harm.

# The institutional capacity and mandate of the Ministry of Water Resources and Meteorology (MOWRAM) is strengthened from a sub-national delivery to integrate water resources management with climate change adaptation and mitigation.

CARE aims to promote equitable access to rural finance via Village Savings and Loan Associations (VSLA) and Poverty Reduction and Livelihoods Targeting (PRTL). Linking households to credit, insurance and legal markets. The project will also support the development of a national VSLA network. The project is focused on providing investment, thus reducing dependence on unreliable and expensive sources of finance.

## 1. Through capacity for policy reform, this project aims to help to reduce disaster risk and enhance climate resilience in rural communities by strengthening the capacity of local government and water user associations (WUAs).

CARE aims to develop a pro-poor, sustainable, and climate-resilient agriculture sector through promoting agricultural diversification and protecting biodiversity. This is achieved through the promotion of climate-smart agriculture practices.

## 2. Climate-smart agriculture

Climate-smart agriculture encompasses a strong evidence-based approach to sustainable natural resource management, including water-use efficiency, sustainable transformation and financial inclusion. It is also focused on building resilience to climate change by promoting climate-smart agriculture and water management.

The paradigm shift presented is aimed at enabling the poor to light.

## 1.4 Sustainable development potential

### Scale: Medium to high

#### Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs)

The project aims to contribute to the achievement of the SDGs, specifically:

- **SDG 6** (Clean Water and Sanitation): Improving water quality by reducing pollution, eliminating open defecation, and expanding equitable and safe access to water for all.
- **SDG 13** (Climate Action): Enhancing resilience and adaptive capacity to climate-related hazards and natural disasters in all countries.

#### Advancing real sustainable development

The CARE project is designed to enhance agricultural productivity and increase security to enable farmers. This supports a more resilient agriculture sector, which is essential for the achievement of SDG 6 and contributes to the implementation of SDG 13. It also integrates climate-smart practices into farming systems.

# Sustainability in training

The current training approach is limited to the project manager, with no mechanism to sustain capacity development. The AI should work with managers and other stakeholders to develop a training plan that includes regular updates on MOVESM and support ongoing professional development.

Workshops and training should be designed to introduce MOVESM and its applications to new users and reinforce existing skills. The AI should also introduce a certification framework for workshops and training, ensuring that all participants are properly trained and qualified.

# Green Climate Fund

## Project: Climate-Smart Agriculture

Promotes private investment in climate-resilient agriculture, improving access to finance and market information. Targets CAADP and PAI principles such as farmers' rights, gender equality, and environmental sustainability.

### Objectives:
- Strengthen the capacity of agricultural value chains.
- Enhance the resilience of smallholder farmers.
- Improve the efficiency of agricultural markets through better integration of climate-smart agriculture (CSA) practices.

## Robust economic and financial analysis
The CAADP project demonstrates strong potential for private sector investment in climate-smart agriculture. The project is expected to generate significant benefits, including increased productivity and profitability for farmers, as well as improved food security and nutrition. The project also aims to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by promoting sustainable agricultural practices.

### Key outcomes:
- A 15% increase in farmer income.
- A 20% reduction in post-harvest losses.
- A 30% increase in the adoption of climate-smart agriculture practices.

## Project benefits reduced
A 10% per cent reduction in benefits (due to lower output prices) reduces the NPV by US$ 5.8 million, but does not change the IRR or payback period. The project remains profitable even if the benefit is reduced by 20-71 per cent before the project becomes unviable.

## Cost increases
A 10% increase in costs is likely to reduce benefits significantly and may make the project unprofitable. However, the project can still be viable with a cost increase of up to 30%.

## Shorter evaluation periods
Reducing the assessment period to 25 years drops the IRR to 18.6 per cent and the NPV to US$ 47.9 million. This highlights the importance of long-term investment in climate-smart agriculture.

## Benefit delays
A one-year delay has minimal impact, but a three-year delay slightly reduces benefits by around 5%.

## Sectoral disruptions rate changes
A lower 6 per cent rate rather than the NPV 10% x3.87 million benefit reduction is likely to have a minor impact on the project's viability.

## Adoption rate changes
If farmer adoption of new practices falls from 75 to 50 per cent, the IRR drops to 24.6 per cent and the NPV to US$ 139.8 million. This highlights the importance of farmer training and support for successful project implementation.

## Extension of environmental benefits
Increasing GDP assumptions reduces the IRR from 27.5 to 20.8 per cent and the NPV by US$ 46.8 million, but the project remains profitable.

## Limited irrigation expansion
If new irrigated areas are added post-project, the IRR increases to 31.9 per cent and the NPV to US$ 175.2 million.

## Risk to efficiency and effectiveness

### Increase GHG emissions

The project's GHG emissions are due to the increased use of fossil fuels. This is a result of the reduced financial capacity of the CARB to implement the project. The CARB will be required to increase its GHG emissions by 5% per year through 2030. This is due to the reduction in GHG emissions in the CARB.

### No need rehabilitation projects

If the project's rehabilitation is not completed, it is excluded as one of the benefits components. The rehabilitation measure will be implemented at a cost of $10 million per year for the next 5 years. This is equivalent to $20 million per year (or $40 million per year) for the next 5 years.

### Limited assessment of climate extreme events

The risks of climate variability are not fully assessed. The project's financial capacity is limited by the lack of a comprehensive risk management plan that could be partially covered due to the lack of a comprehensive risk management plan. This is due to the lack of a comprehensive risk management plan that could be partially covered due to the lack of a comprehensive risk management plan.

### 10
The I-TAP recommends that the Board approve the funding proposal, given the key requirements of I-TAP and the recommendations of GMAA project cycle.

To address the operational GCP constraints, sub-national risks or unanticipated changes in the environment, a risk management plan should be prepared. This includes:

- A risk register (R-15).
- A risk management plan (RMP) should be registered with the following on a continuous basis:
  - Risk identification.
  - Risk assessment.
  - Risk mitigation.

### 12
Deferral of the GCP to the Accredited Entity, as a first and immediate consequence of the conditions provided by the I-TAP.

- Environmental and social impact assessment.
- Development impact assessment.
- Environmental and social management plans.
- Environmental and social monitoring reports.
- Environmental and social audits.

### 14

- A results framework.

- A mid-term review.
- An evaluation plan.

### 15

- An environmental and social impact assessment.
- A development impact assessment.
- Environmental and social management plans.
- Environmental and social monitoring reports.
- Environmental and social audits.

# Introduction

## 1.1 Project Background

### 1.1.1 Project Objective

The project aims to enhance climate adaptation, mitigate the negative impact of extreme climate events, and improve livelihoods of unsheltered farmers and vulnerable rural communities in four provinces of Cambodia. Being a low-lying country, Cambodia is at risk from rising sea levels. These objectives will be achieved by implementing three components that aim at addressing climate change vulnerabilities, increasing agricultural productivity, and enhancing water management and agricultural practices at the farm level. (i) establish climate-proof irrigation and flood control infrastructure; (ii) enhance community preparedness and response to climate risks; and (iii) improve water management and agricultural practices under a changing climate context.

The project objective is to enhance climate adaptation, mitigate the negative impact of extreme climate events, and improve livelihoods of unsheltered farmers and vulnerable rural communities in four provinces of Cambodia, including Pursat, Kampong Chhnang, Takeo, and Kandal communities (see map below).

![Map showing six irrigation sub-schemes located in four provinces](image_url)

Page 143


---
**Page 158**
---

# 1.2.2 Approach

The GAFSP project aims to bring about benefits to 100,000 households (100,000 people), 3% of the population. The project will be executed through the Ministry of Water Resources and Meteorology (MOWRAM) and the Department of Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries (DAFF). A Project Management Unit (PMU) will be established under the DAFF to ensure effective implementation of the project activities. The PMU will report directly to the GAFSP.

## 1.1.2 Project Activities

The project will carry out a wide range of activities in the four project provinces. These activities are organized as follows:

### Component 1: Increase farm-level climate-smart agriculture, resilience, and water efficiency

#### Output 1.1.1 Increased farmer capacity in climate-resilient agriculture

This output will focus on developing farmers' capacities in applying Climate-Smart Agriculture (CSA) technologies and practices to increase their productivity and income. The project will train farmers through the Climate-Smart Agriculture Action Plans (APs) to improve farm management and assist them in transferring the knowledge gained from training to their own agricultural activities.

- **Activity 1.1.1.1** Preparation of community-based action plans (APs) in collaboration with CSA practitioners
- **Activity 1.1.1.2** Preparation of training materials to support implementation of APs
- **Activity 1.1.1.3** Conduct training to create a pool of resourceful demonstrators and expose the CSA technologies and practices
- **Activity 1.1.1.4** Training and advisory on CSA technologies and practices

#### Output 1.1.2 Increased adaptive value chains developed by APs and increased access to finance

This output focuses on supporting and enhancing value chains that are key to the project areas and strengthening farmers' capacities in accessing markets and financial services. The project will support partnerships (APs) and increased access to finance, which will improve market access, climate adaptability, and resilience for communities in the value chain.

- **Activity 1.2.1** Establish District Multi-Stakeholder Platforms (MSPs)
- **Activity 1.2.2** Develop Product Partnership Facility (PPF)
