# Output 1.3: Improved access to and use of climate information and advisory services for climate responsive agriculture

This is a three-component all-inclusive approach in the production and dissemination of climate agrometeorological information to ensure climate responsive management and planning of applications in the project target areas.

- Activity 1.3.1 Strengthening of agro-meteorological station in the project area
    - Output 1.3.1.1 Establishment of a fully automated weather station
    - Output 1.3.1.2 Establishment of an agro-meteorological information system

- Activity 1.3.2 Enhancement of climate advisory services
    - Output 1.3.2.1 Establishment of climate advisory services
    - Output 1.3.2.2 Establishment of a climate information service

# Output 1.4: Improved challenge of farm road infrastructure in climate change

- Activity 1.4.1 Identification, selection and design planning of farm roads
    - Output 1.4.1.1 Design of 50 kilometers of farm roads
- Activity 1.4.2 Implementation of the designed farm roads
    - Output 1.4.2.1 Construction of 50 kilometers of farm roads

# Component 2: Integrated Infrastructure for improved resilience

Component 2 is linked with Component 3 (both part of) to facilitate the implementation of CR on farm crop and livestock management systems. It includes the establishment of climate resilient infrastructure such as soil and water conservation structures, drainage channels, irrigation systems, and other related infrastructure. The use of sub-projects will be integrated into the overall project design to ensure climate resilient integrated agriculture systems for adapting to both increasing flood and drought conditions.

# Output 2.1: Modernized irrigation schemes and stands

- Activity 2.1.1 Technical analysis, field surveys and preparation of plans for system upgrading
    - Output 2.1.1.1 Preparation of plans for system upgrading
- Activity 2.1.3 Preparation of capital O&M plans including specification of ICT and SMADM for operation
    - Output 2.1.3.1 Preparation of capital O&M plans

# Output 2.2: Flood proof and improved drainage

- Activity 2.2.1 Technical analysis, field surveys and preparation of plans for system upgrading
    - Output 2.2.1.1 Preparation of plans for system upgrading
- Activity 2.2.3 Implementation of a comprehensive flood control drainage system
    - Output 2.2.3.1 Implementation of a comprehensive flood control drainage system

# Output 2.3: Improved Water Grid (PVC) established and utilized

- Activity 2.3.1 Technical analysis, field surveys and preparation of plans for system upgrading
    - Output 2.3.1.1 Preparation of plans for system upgrading
- Activity 2.3.3 Establishment of a comprehensive water grid system
    - Output 2.3.3.1 Establishment of a comprehensive water grid system

# Output 2.4: Integrated information system (ISWS and GISAD) established and operational in sub-projects

- Activity 2.4.1 Feasibility of premises for establishment of the ISWS and Water Information System
    - Output 2.4.1.1 Feasibility study report

# Committee 3: Environmental Transitions

## Output 3.1 Strengthened NWRM/C Concepts

- **Activity 3.1.1** Strengthening River Basin Management and Water Accounting Systems
- **Activity 3.1.2** Communicating with Stakeholders
- **Activity 3.1.3** Support to the Design of Courses, Village and FARMING and Gender Awareness

## Output 3.2 Strengthened NCCDS at national and provincial level

- **Activity 3.2.1** Strengthening NCCDS at the national level
- **Activity 3.2.2** Support to the Design of Courses, Village and FARMING and Gender Awareness
  - **Sub-activity 3.2.2.1** Project's Evaluation and Social Risks and Impacts
    While the project will bring about an overall positive, long-term social and environmental impact, some project activities, particularly those under Component 1 (Operating and Closing Water Infrastructure for Irrigation), may have negative impacts on local communities.
    During project implementation, the following environmental risks and impacts are anticipated - at specific construction sites, due to implementation of some of the above mentioned project activities.

### Environmental Risks and Impacts

- **Groundwater Depletion**
  - Groundwater depletion is a potential impact from the use of water (irrigation, self-change of use, etc.).
  - Generation of solid hazardous waste (removal during construction stage).
    - Solid waste generated by the project will be disposed of in accordance with environmental regulations.
      - In the long term, the project may cause changes to the groundwater table.
- **Air Quality**
  - Air quality impacts are expected from the use of diesel generators and other equipment ("worksite" activities).
  - Dust emissions during construction activities (e.g., ground-breaking, excavation, etc.).
    - Unpaved roads (associated with physical construction activities that involve ground-breaking).

Most of these risks and impacts are temporary, localized and short-lived. Mitigation measures for their environmental risks and impacts are being proposed to avoid these impacts, including measures through the project's implementation plan.
- **Noise**
  - Noise pollution is expected during the construction phase - based on the following activities:
    - Technical standards and regulations must be applied by the contractor.
    - The project's measures must be environmentally and socio-economically feasible.
    - The project will monitor noise levels to ensure compliance with environmental regulations.
      - Monitoring activities must be conducted on a regular basis.

**Page 161**

# Social Risks and Impacts

During project implementation, the following social risks and impacts are anticipated - at specific construction stages:

- **Patriarchal land acquisition (mostly local people for rehabilitation/construction of irrigation canal)**
  - Landless poor (especially women) may be affected by land acquisition.
  - Gender inequality (e.g., during the process of land compensation).
  - Sexual exploitation and Abuse (SEA): Sex has assumed GNI, Gender Based Violence (GBV) due to labor migration.

- **Exclusion of vulnerable groups (e.g. ethnic minorities, poor, people with disabilities, homeless, etc.)**
  - Lack of access to basic services.
  - Displacement and resettlement issues.
  - Labor migration (used to lower labor cost and exploitation that is useful for certain districts).
  - Road and traffic safety (during the implementation activities along construction).

# 1.2 Mitigation Measures

To ensure project’s risks and impacts are well managed/mitigated, the project has prepared the following:

- **Resettlement Policy Framework (RPF)**
- **Gender Action Plan (GAP)**
- **Stakeholder Engagement Plan (SEP)**
- **Gender Mainstreaming including Gender Action Plan**
- **Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA)**

The project will be guided by the principles of the AIH (Environmental and Social Framework), IADB (Development Policy), and relevant policies of all line agencies in the Philippines. The project will also consider the following:

- The above documents are prepared to ensure environmental and social risks and impacts are well managed.
- The project will promote gender equality, gender equity, and women’s empowerment throughout the project design, pre-construction, during construction, and operational stages.

# 12 Results of Gender Assessment

The Gender Assessment is undertaken to understand the status quo for gender related issues in the area of infrastructure/protection/irrigation management in Canada, particularly at project level. Based on these understandings, activities for gender mainstreaming will be prepared, put into motion and evaluated through the following:

- **Gender Action Plan (GAP)**
  - The GAP will identify gender gaps through engaging the stakeholders.
  - The GAP will also include a monitoring framework to ensure that the identified gender issues are addressed.

The Gender Assessment will be guided by an integral member with experience in project management activities.

# The gender action plan aims to:

1. Support (gender) equality and empowerment throughout the humanitarian sector by ensuring that at least 40% of the humanitarian field staff are women across key functions.
2. Promote greater equity through chronic change integration and adaptation interventions where necessary, including in the areas of recruitment, retention, and promotion.
3. Contribute to reducing the gender gap caused by inherent social, economic, and environmental pressures and actions, as well as potential impacts reduced by the climate change in our project programming.

## 2. METHODS

### 2.1 Research Design
#### 2.1.1 Data Collection
Most quantitative data collection described below were conducted in 2022 by the Livelihoods and Vulnerability Services from communities that are vulnerable and most at risk.

- **FGDs (Focus Group Discussions)**: 8 FGDs gathered data from ordinary households, ethnic groups, natural resource dependent and experts to discuss pre-existing gender norms and practices. Each FGD had 10 participants.
- **KIIs (Key Informant Interviews)**: 3 KIIs were guided by individuals who are trusted governmental staff. The group facilitators included local leaders, community members, and local government officials.

| No. | Date       | Community | Method      | Number of participants | Total |
|-----|------------|-----------|-------------|-----------------------|-------|
| 1   | June 2023  | -         | FGDs        | 80 (40%)              | 160   |

### Key Informant Interview (KII)
KII aims to collect information about direct pressing issues and problems in the target community – through individuals who are actively known as experts in the fields under discussion, and who have a good understanding of the local context. KIIs were conducted with a focus on a quick check that are likely to be supported by the project, about one hundred such informant interviews were conducted by the livelihoods and vulnerability survey team.

Focus group discussions (FGDs) were conducted through personal mobile devices of local government staff who conducted the surveys.

# RESULTS OF GENDER ASSESSMENT

## 3.1 Overview of Country Level

### 3.1.1 Background on gender issues

Cambodia ranked fifth with respect to the Global Gender Gap Index 2021 ratings. This is an improvement compared to the previous rankings where it was ranked sixth, seventh, and eighth in the years 2018, 2019, and 2020 respectively. The country has made significant progress over the past decade with respect to closing gender gaps in health, survival, and education. Back in history, traditional customs (Chbap Srey/Pailin) have been known to discriminate against women. However, women are now more empowered and have greater control over their lives, wealth, and labor force participation. This has led to an increase in the number of women who are now actively participating in decision-making processes at home and in society.

For women and men who have never heard of Chbap Srey/Pailin, the concept is explained as follows: "Chbap Srey" refers to the idea that women should be submissive and obedient. The term "Pailin" refers to the idea that women should not be too loud or assertive. These are cultural norms that shape the gender roles in the next generation. The death described in Chbap Srey/Pailin is a metaphor for the traditional gender norms that have been passed down through generations.

The findings of this assessment indicate that women's empowerment has been strengthened at various levels, including through policies and other structural measures to ensure equal opportunities for men and women. Women's entrepreneurship has also increased significantly in recent years, especially in urban areas (e.g., SMEs and micro-enterprises).

Women are traditionally responsible for household chores including care-giving control. When it comes to issues such as seeking out health care and income generation, women take on the responsibility of seeking out local credit and income greater responsibility for their families.

The COVID-19 pandemic has had a significant impact on gender equality in Cambodia. According to the World Bank (2021), women are more likely to be affected by unemployment and care work in which they are engaged, such as the work during COVID-19 pandemic.

proportion of men and women went increased time on domestic work affairs about half of employed women and men in Canada. Women are more likely to be involved in domestic work than men. The need to maintain a household is one reason for this difference. Women also tend to be more involved in the care of children, the elderly, or other dependents. This is consistent with social norms that emphasize women's roles as caregivers and nurturers. It is also related to broader issues such as gender inequality and cultural norms that reinforce traditional gender roles.

Women assume a wider range of domestic and nondomestic roles than men. They are more likely to be involved in both paid employment and unpaid household work, including child care and elder care. This double burden can lead to increased stress, fatigue, and health problems. Women who work outside the home often have less time for leisure activities and may rely on their partners or other family members to help with domestic chores.

The Canadian labor force participation (LFP) rate for both the male and female population (aged 15 to 64) is high compared to other countries in the OECD region. Canadians (LP) rates are similar to those of Vermont, New Zealand, and Australia. The LFP rate for women has increased significantly over time, reflecting changes in societal norms and policies that support women's participation in the labor force. This includes greater access to education and employment opportunities.

The proportion of women in Canada who work full-time is higher than the OECD average. In 2019, about 75% of employed women worked full-time, compared to around 86% of men. The gender gap in full-time employment has narrowed over time, but there are still significant differences between men and women.

The proportion of women in Canada who work full-time is higher than the OECD average. In 2019, about 75% of employed women worked full-time, compared to around 86% of men. The gender gap in full-time employment has narrowed over time, but there are still significant differences between men and women.

# Gender-based division of labor in rural Cambodia

By 2013, women made up 52 percent of the Cambodian population for 1.7 million who migrated. Of the total migration, 64% were rural-to-rural accounts for 94%, followed by rural-urban (25%), mostly to Phnom Penh and urban-urban (1%). Men dominated rural-rural migration (80%) whereas women dominated rural-urban migration (73%).

Men migrated to Phnom Penh for work in garment factories (32.6%), small businesses (21.4 percent), domestic work (15.9%), construction (14.4%), and other activities (16%). Women migrated to Phnom Penh for work in garment factories (32.6%), small businesses (21.4 percent), domestic work (15.9%), construction (14.4%), and other activities (16%).

Men migrated to rural areas with a total of 148,998 workers (10.8%) women. Kandal (Otdar Meanchey) and Takeo had the highest number of migrants (32.7%). Men migrated to Phnom Penh for work in garment factories (32.6%), small businesses (21.4 percent), domestic work (15.9%), construction (14.4%), and other activities (16%).

Migration has a strong influence on gender-based division of labor in rural Cambodia. This affects the level of participation of women in agriculture, which is characterized by the fear of increasing migration of young men from rural areas to urban areas. Migration also affects the level of participation of women in agricultural activities and duties, a key driving factor for rural people to migrate, leaving children, women, and elderly behind.

One of the few Cambodian women have experienced physical or sexual violence from an intimate partner, and more than half of these women did not seek help. Women who experience violence are less likely to participate in gender-based violence. Physical and sexual violence is a major cause of gender-based violence. During the COVID-19 pandemic, the prevalence rate increased by 30% among women.

Research has shown that rural households affect women more than domestic, partner violence. Public spaces and public transport are common sites for rural harassment. Studies on rural harassment found that 87% of women experienced sexual harassment in public places such as markets, bus stops, and other public areas.

### Recommendations by a climate change initiative and women's economic empowerment

The 6th IAAI & Gender Equality Quality Ministerial Meeting entitled "Building Resilience to Economic Crises and Moving Forward," held in Rome, Italy on November 2017, made the following specific recommendations for promoting gender equality:

- Women entrepreneurs: Mathematics (like a woman's business associations) should be strengthened to promote entrepreneurship, access to business-related information, green technology and financial services.
- Organic and sustainable agriculture: the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries should develop a program to support women farmers in organic farming and provide them with technical assistance and training on sustainable farming practices.
- Green employment: Public sector procurement should prioritize women-owned businesses and small enterprises. The government should also promote green jobs through public works programs in schools, hospitals, and enterprises. Priority should be given to women's participation in these programs.
- Public-Private Partnerships (PPPs): Promotion of PPPs will be a key strategy for promoting green employment. Women's economic empowerment should be integrated into the social impact assessment process. Women's economic empowerment should also be integrated into the development of green jobs and public works programs.
- Women's economic empowerment: Women's economic empowerment should be integrated into the development of green jobs and public works programs. Women should be given access to credit, training, and technical assistance. Women should have equal opportunities for employment in all sectors of the economy. Women should also have access to productive resources, information and extension services.

# Gender Mainstreaming Guidance Manual (2017)

MDVA's Gender Mainstreaming Guidance Manual (2017) has reviewed the progress of national gender mainstreaming efforts in the Philippines and identified key challenges. These are summarized by MDVA in a table below.

| Type of Gender Mainstreaming | Progress | Challenges |
|-----------------------------|----------|------------|
| **Government-wide**         | There is a national policy on gender equality and women's empowerment (GEWE) and other laws that promote GEWE. The Philippine Plan for Gender-Responsive Development 1998-2025 (PPGD) has been adopted by the government as the framework for mainstreaming GEWE in all sectors of society. | There is no specific law on gender equality and women's empowerment. |
| **Sectoral**                | The National Commission on the Role of Filipino Women (NCRFW) has been established to promote and protect the rights of women. The Philippine Plan for Gender-Responsive Development 1998-2025 (PPGD) has been adopted by the government as the framework for mainstreaming GEWE in all sectors of society. | There is no specific law on gender equality and women's empowerment. |
| **Community**               | The NCRFW has been established to promote and protect the rights of women. The Philippine Plan for Gender-Responsive Development 1998-2025 (PPGD) has been adopted by the government as the framework for mainstreaming GEWE in all sectors of society. | There is no specific law on gender equality and women's empowerment. |
| **Gender Mainstreaming in the Health Sector** | The Department of Health (DOH) has developed a Gender and Development (GAD) Plan for 2017-2022. The plan aims to promote gender equality and empower women in the health sector. | There is no specific law on gender equality and women's empowerment. |
| **Gender Mainstreaming in the Education Sector** | The Department of Education (DepEd) has developed a Gender and Development (GAD) Plan for 2017-2022. The plan aims to promote gender equality and empower women in the education sector. | There is no specific law on gender equality and women's empowerment. |
| **Gender Mainstreaming in the Labor Sector** | The Department of Labor and Employment (DOLE) has developed a Gender and Development (GAD) Plan for 2017-2022. The plan aims to promote gender equality and empower women in the labor sector. | There is no specific law on gender equality and women's empowerment. |

## 3.1 National Policy related to Gender & Climate Change

### National/Regional Instruments
The government has adopted a legal framework that aims to promote the role of women as part of national efforts to address climate change. The Climate Change Act of 2009 (Republic Act No. 9729) recognizes the importance of gender equality and the empowerment of women in disaster risk reduction and climate change adaptation. The law also provides for the establishment of a Gender and Development (GAD) Committee within the Climate Change Commission to ensure that gender issues are considered in all climate change-related policies, programs, and projects.

### International Instruments
The government has ratified various international instruments on climate change and gender equality, including the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), the Kyoto Protocol, and the Paris Agreement. The government has also adopted the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction 2015-2030.

The law also provides for the establishment of a Gender and Development (GAD) Committee within the Climate Change Commission to ensure that gender issues are considered in all climate change-related policies, programs, and projects.

### International Instruments
The government has ratified various international instruments on climate change and gender equality, including the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), the Kyoto Protocol, and the Paris Agreement. The government has also adopted the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction 2015-2030.

# Annex to the Convention

In terms of international convention, the Royal Government of Cambodia has ratified 13 International Labor Organization conventions (see table below). Cambodia is also a signatory to various United Nations human rights instruments and other international treaties on women's and children's rights. The government has established the National Committee for Women, which was set up in 1979. However, it is noted that the C86 - Night Work (Youthful Persons) Convention, 1937 (No. 50), has not been ratified by a force.

## Conventions

| Convention | Date       | Status    |
|-------------|------------|-----------|
| C029        | Convention (Revision) of the Hours of Work in Industry Convention, 1919, 1937 No. 4 | 25 Aug 1999 | In Force |
| C068        | Hours of Work (Industry) Convention, 1947 No. 61 | 25 Aug 1999 | In Force |
| C081        | Weekly Rest (Industry) Convention, 1921 No. 3 | 25 Aug 1999 | In Force |
| C111        | Discrimination (Employment and Occupation) Convention, 1958 No. 111 | 25 Aug 1999 | In Force |
| C122        | Minimum Age Convention, 1973 No. 138 | 25 Aug 1999 | In Force |
| C138        | Minimum Age (Industry) Convention, 1973 No. 141 | 25 Aug 1999 | In Force |
| C066        | Night Work of Young Persons (Industry) Convention, 1937 No. 4 | 24 Feb 1978 | Not in Force |
| C090        | Labor Inspection (Agriculture) Convention, 1935 No. 33 | 25 Aug 1999 | In Force |
| C136        | Labor Inspection (Industry) Convention, 1981 No. 148 | 25 Aug 1999 | In Force |

Despite the presence of anti-discrimination legislation, there is no provision for equal remuneration for work of equal value. The law does not prohibit discrimination in employment on the basis of gender and has been applied to the government, private sector, and small businesses. The industrial sector employs a large number of female workers who are employed as domestic servants.

In terms of land ownership, Land Law (2001) stipulates that women have equal rights to land ownership. Overall, the law provides for equal pay for men and women in both the public and private sectors. However, gender discrimination is still widespread and women often face obstacles when they try to claim their rights.

## Cambodia's Agricultural Strategy (Phase II)

The Government of Cambodia's Agricultural Strategy Phase II (2018-2023) provides a framework for achieving sustainable agricultural development through increased productivity, employment opportunities for youth, and strengthening the competitiveness of the agricultural sector. The strategy aims to improve the livelihoods of farmers by increasing their income and promoting sustainable farming practices.

# Master Plan on Gender and Climate Change (2018-2030)

## Caribbean Climate Change Horizon (2017-2057)
The impacts of climate change are already being felt across the region. Not only does climate change have direct impacts on the natural environment, but it also affects people's lives and livelihoods through changes in agriculture, energy and water resources, health, tourism, fisheries, and coastal zones. In the Caribbean, the impacts of climate change are most acute for agriculture, and the risk is mainly local communities. As the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events increase, so does the vulnerability to disasters.

The RBC recognizes that the rural poor of the Caribbean, the majority of whom are women, are most vulnerable to the adverse effects of climate change. The region's small island developing states (SIDS) are particularly vulnerable to climate risks and shocks. There is a need to mainstream gender into climate risk management strategies and policies. Gender-responsive climate risk management is essential for building resilience and reducing vulnerability to climate change.

The RBC recognizes that the rural poor of the Caribbean, the majority of whom are women, are most vulnerable to the adverse effects of climate change. The region's small island developing states (SIDS) are particularly vulnerable to climate risks and shocks. There is a need to mainstream gender into climate risk management strategies and policies. Gender-responsive climate risk management is essential for building resilience and reducing vulnerability to climate change.

Gender, all policies, strategies and programs dealing with climate change are either about climate change adaptation or climate change mitigation. It is important to draw distinction between the two. Climate change adaptation refers to adjustments in natural or human systems over time to avoid the negative effects of climate change. Mitigation refers to efforts to reduce or prevent emissions and enhance activities that remove greenhouse gases from the atmosphere. The sweet spot of climate change adaptation is guided by and contributes to the Millennium Development Goals (MDGs) on poverty, vulnerability and adaptation, a program developed by the UN Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC).

# Convention for Climate Change (UNFCCC)

Convention for Climate Change (UNFCCC) is helping contain understood climate change impacts and adapt to them.

Climate change is expected to compound and amplify development challenges, stresses, and vulnerabilities in Canada, further affecting poor and marginalized people, particularly women and children. Women have a critical role to play in addressing climate change and are often the primary providers of development services that are essential for effective adaptation to climate change. The vast majority of women's organizations are local and community-based, with deep knowledge, experience, and understanding of their communities' needs and priorities.

## 2.1 Existing Implementation Arrangements

The Convention supports women's empowerment through various mechanisms including the Ministry of Women's Affairs (MOWA) in Afghanistan, the National Gender Policy and Action Plan (GAP), and the Millennium Development Goal (MDG) indicators on gender equality. The Convention also supports the implementation of other MDGs in priority areas such as health, education, and poverty reduction. The plan prioritizes three areas of focus: (i) promoting women's empowerment; (ii) enhancing women's participation in decision-making processes at all levels from the household to the local, national, regional, and international levels; and (iii) ensuring gender equality and equity.

The Convention also supports the implementation of the Millennium Development Goals (MDGs), including the Millennium Declaration, which includes specific goals for women. The Convention supports the implementation of the Millennium Development Goals (MDGs), including the Millennium Declaration, which includes specific goals for women. The Convention supports the implementation of the Millennium Development Goals (MDGs), including the Millennium Declaration, which includes specific goals for women.

### 2.1.1 Gender Training Programs

Women's organizations are providing training programs in areas such as household production, handicrafts, and food processing. These programs help women develop skills that can be used to generate income and improve their standard of living. The Convention supports the implementation of these programs and includes environmental sustainability training, business development services, and current labor market trends.

### 2.1.2 Gender Mainstreaming

The Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry, and Fisheries (MAFF) has prepared a Gender Mainstreaming Policy and Action Plan to promote gender equality in agriculture. The Convention supports the implementation of this policy and action plan. The Convention also supports the implementation of other gender mainstreaming policies and programs, including those that focus on women's empowerment, gender equality, and sustainable development.

# 1.2 Current practices

Most households (90%, n=3187) have used traditional farming tools over the past five decades for better income. The rural labor force has been reduced, showing room for increased use of machinery to compensate for traditional human labor. The use of modern machinery is still low due to high costs and lack of awareness.

- 12% (n=438) have used tractors.
- 65.3% (n=2079) have used traditional farming tools like hoes, sickles, etc.
- 22.7% (n=782) have used improved farming technologies.

Most households (65%, n=1104) have been using traditional rice cultivation practices with a lag in adopting new farming techniques. 21.7% (n=369) use electricity, and 13.6% (n=238) use modern farming equipment. Improved farming technologies are better for crop yields.

- 50.4% (n=863) have used traditional farming practices.
- 38.7% (n=662) have used improved farming techniques.
- 10.9% (n=188) have used modern farming technologies.

## Climate change and new technologies

Most surveyed households (80%, n=548) are not aware of the potential impact of climate change on their agricultural production. However, 20% (n=136) think they have experienced the impact of climate change and are using new technologies to adapt.

## Training needs

When asked about the importance of training in improving their farming practices:

- 87.5% (n=498) think it is very important.
- 12.5% (n=70) think it is somewhat important.

Most households (86%, n=378) know what they need to do to support their household development.

# The Impact of Microfinance on Women

The impacts that both income and non-income benefits are usually hard to separate because they overlap and interact with each other. According to the study, women who were part of a microfinance group had better health status and dry season savings compared to those not in such groups. In particular, during wet seasons, key expenses include income losses (10.4%), investment in agricultural production (9.5%), housing (2.4%), and less cultivation (3.6%).

Key reasons women give for joining microfinance include income losses than expenditures (18%), children's education (14.8%), social expenses (13.4%), medical care (10.4%), and drought damages (9.7%). Other expenses include loss of organization value (6.8%) and money for rentier (5.2%).

61.1% of respondents have had no experience working in a community farming group, 68.1% are not involved in any group activities at all/involuntary labor. Most women in the country do their own work and do not participate in any group activities. The majority of them are housewives who have no other choice since women are underrepresented by banks.

## 2.3 Loan Use

According to the Cambodia Microfinance Association (CMFAL, 90% of these clients are women. Loans used for business purposes are rapidly well-witnessed interest rate higher than commercial banks.

Women use microfinance loans to purchase household items such as a fan or rice from a private shop. The purpose of the loan is to buy food and other necessities. Most of them will buy a loan (see graph below). Most of these women take a loan from a legitimate bank, and not the local bank.

# Household Characteristics

The households with less than 1 ha of cropland is characterized by the following:

- Average net size is lower than that of those having more than 1 ha of cropland*
- Average age of household head is higher than that of those having more than 1 ha of cropland*
- Average percentage of monthly food expenditure is higher
- Average percentage of non-food expenditure is lower
- More likely to borrow loan compared to those having more than 1 ha of cropland*

## Gender Analysis

### Labor Division

For those who are involved in farming as their main household activities, virtually all of them (97.6%, n=451) have male household heads. Among the female counterparts who stand 3.0% of their total farming, the following tasks are assigned to women:

- **First stage of rice cultivation:** seedbed preparation and transplanting
- **Second stage of rice cultivation:** weeding and harvesting
- **Other activities:** planting, weeding, and marketing

generally shared task," however, given the migration of one of her better income, emancipation has become a major issue for many women in the region. The migration of women from rural areas to urban centers has led to an increase in prostitution, illegal activities, and the spread of HIV/AIDS. In addition, the lack of access to education and health care services has exacerbated the problem.

In fact, most rural women (70%) are involved in farming, livestock such as chickens and ducks, and pig-raising. They also engage in small-scale trade, selling vegetables, fruits, and other agricultural products. However, as mentioned in Cambodia's labor market literature, poor households often rely on the income of their children to support the family. In addition, women are often responsible for caring for elderly parents, which can limit their ability to work outside the home.

The COVID-19 pandemic has also exacerbated these issues, with some areas experiencing even increased time on household and care work. Elders who were previously able to raise children while mothers have migrated for work now face additional challenges. In addition, migrants for paid work, domestic workers, and those in the informal sector are particularly vulnerable to job loss and poverty. According to the National Institute of Statistics (NIS), unemployed households were more than twice as likely to be poor and food insecure compared to employed households.

### 2.3 Income

At the individual level, the average income of all female household members is $121.4 USD per month (+/-236.5), with a median of $70.00. The average monthly income between male and female members is $63.2 USD, and a statistically significant difference was found (p<0.05). In addition, the average household size is 4.10 people.

In terms of age, according to the National Demographic and Health Survey (NDHS) 2019-2021, when men were asked about their main decision-making regarding how they spend money, most reported 15-30 years old (67%), followed by 40-49 years old (18%). In addition, women aged 15-30 years old are more likely to make decisions than those in other age groups. In fact, 97% of married women aged 15-30 can make each decision independently.

# Skills of female household members

In the option of the surveyed households, women should have the following education, followed by freedom of decision-making, good husband, income opportunities, and new living.

## Access to Agricultural Extension Service

It is estimated that only 10 percent of the Cambodian farmers with access to agricultural extension service are female. The main reasons for this gender gap in agricultural extension services include distance to the point of service provision; incompatibility to the level of literacy; time constraints due to household responsibilities; and traditional gender roles. Women farmers have limited access to agricultural extension services traditionally registered in household head and are often automatically listed as dependents.

## Market access

Women face a number of challenges that impede their ability, lower level of literacy, less access to land, less assets than men, less access to credit, less access to information, and less access to markets. Women farmers are identified as the main gender gap in Cambodia to access to markets for women.

# GENDER ACTION & SOCIAL INCLUSION PLAN

From the above analysis, it is clear with regards to participation of women in farming activities and household chores have been identified. To reduce the gender gap, it is important that barriers to relieving the gender gap be addressed. The following are some of the strategies to address this issue:

- **Women's access to land**: Women should have equal access to land for farming.
- **Women's access to credit**: Access to credit facilities will enable women to invest in agriculture and improve their livelihoods.
- **Women's participation in decision-making processes**: Women should be involved in decision-making processes at all levels, including household, community, and national levels.

## 4.1 Key Barriers to Participation of Women

This section highlights the key barriers that have been identified as preventing women from participating fully in agriculture activities:

### Barrier 1: Gender Norms

Gender norms are deeply rooted in social norms where long-standing traditions and values dictate the responsibilities and roles for household chores and economic generation activities. This affects women's participation in farming activities, as they are often denied access to land ownership and decision-making processes.

Women are expected to perform domestic duties such as cooking, cleaning, and caring for children and elderly family members. These expectations limit their ability to participate fully in agricultural activities. Additionally, cultural norms often dictate that men should be the primary breadwinners, which can lead to women being excluded from economic opportunities.

### Barrier 2: Lack of Access to Resources

Women face significant barriers in accessing resources such as land, credit, and technology. These barriers are exacerbated by gender norms and traditional practices that limit women's access to these resources. For example:

- **Land ownership**: Women often do not have the legal right to own or inherit land.
- **Credit access**: Banks and financial institutions may be reluctant to lend to women due to cultural biases and lack of collateral.
- **Technology adoption**: Women may not have access to modern agricultural technologies, such as tractors, irrigation systems, and fertilizers.

### Barrier 3: Time Constraints

Women face significant time constraints due to their dual roles in household management and farming activities. The burden of household chores often leaves women with little time for rest or leisure, let alone participation in community activities or decision-making processes.

The gender norms define that domestic works are key women's responsibility. Traditional codes (Chieftaincy) have been adopted for several centuries; the code specifies men as head of the household and women as subordinates. This has led to the continued exclusion of women from decision-making processes at all levels, including household, community, and national levels.

Women are often responsible for a wide range of activities, including farming, cooking, cleaning, childcare, and other domestic duties. These responsibilities can be time-consuming and leave little time for rest or leisure. Additionally, women may face discrimination in the workplace, which can limit their ability to advance in their careers and earn a living wage.

### Barrier 4: Lack of Education and Training

Women often lack access to education and training opportunities, which can limit their ability to participate fully in agricultural activities. For example:

- **Literacy rates**: Women may have lower literacy rates than men, which can make it difficult for them to access information and resources.
- **Training programs**: Women may not have access to agricultural training programs, which can limit their ability to adopt new technologies and farming practices.

Women's lack of education and training can also limit their ability to advocate for themselves and challenge gender norms. Without access to education and training, women may be less likely to know their rights and how to assert them.

### Barrier 5: Cultural Practices

Cultural practices can also limit women's participation in agricultural activities. For example:

- **Early marriage**: Early marriage can limit a woman's ability to participate in education and economic activities.
- **Son preference**: The preference for sons over daughters can lead to discrimination against girls and limit their access to resources.

These cultural practices can perpetuate gender norms and limit women's ability to participate fully in agricultural activities. Additionally, these practices can contribute to the cycle of poverty by limiting women's ability to earn a living wage and invest in their families' future.

### Barrier 6: Lack of Political Representation

Women often lack political representation, which can limit their ability to advocate for policies that support their participation in agricultural activities. For example:

- **Political participation**: Women may be underrepresented in political institutions, which can limit their ability to influence policy decisions.
- **Policy advocacy**: Women may not have the resources or networks to advocate for policies that support their participation in agricultural activities.

Without political representation, women may be less likely to have a voice in decision-making processes and advocate for policies that support their participation in agricultural activities. Additionally, lack of political representation can contribute to the perpetuation of gender norms and limit women's ability to challenge these norms.

## Additional Time for Self-Care, Child Development, Learning Opportunities, and New Income Opportunities

Additional time for self-care, child development, learning opportunities, and new income opportunities such as at-home businesses can be achieved through increased female participation in wage-earning activities.

### Opportunity 1 - Female Participation in Household Decision Making

Women's participation in household decision making is a key indicator of gender equality. Women who participate in household decisions have better planning and control over their lives, which leads to improved health outcomes for themselves and their children. Increased female participation in household decision-making has been shown to improve child nutrition, increase the likelihood that girls will receive an education, and reduce domestic violence. It also increases women's ability to participate in community-level activities such as savings groups and microfinance programs.

### Opportunity 2 - Increasing Female Participation in Wage-Earning Activities

Women's participation in wage-earning activities is a key indicator of gender equality. Women who participate in wage-earning activities are more likely to be involved in decision-making processes, have greater control over their own lives, and have better access to resources such as education and health care. Increased female participation in wage-earning activities can lead to improved economic outcomes for women and their families.

### Opportunity 3 - Increasing Female Participation in Wage-Earning Activities

Women's participation in wage-earning activities is a key indicator of gender equality. Women who participate in wage-earning activities are more likely to be involved in decision-making processes, have greater control over their own lives, and have better access to resources such as education and health care. Increased female participation in wage-earning activities can lead to improved economic outcomes for women and their families.

### Opportunity 4 - Project Participation by Grandmothers Raising Orphaned Youth

Grandmothers play a critical role in raising orphaned youth, providing them with the support they need to succeed. The project offers a unique opportunity for grandmothers to participate in project design and implementation, as well as to receive training on how to best support their grandchildren.

### Opportunity 5 - Project Participation by Increased Women Participation in Project Activities

The project offers a unique opportunity where participation of women in project design and implementation is a critical component. This includes the development of new income-generating activities for women, as well as the provision of training and support to help them succeed.

### Priority 1 - Promoting women's participation and leadership in community-based planning decision-making and water governance

Women's participation in community affairs, which is often driven by discriminatory social and cultural norms, are still challenges to be overcome. RUWCs are still heavily male-dominated. Women's participation in water resource management is still limited. Gender equality in water resource management is important for sustainable development. It will require strengthening water governance by addressing its social and gender dimensions. This requires strengthening women's participation in decision-making processes, including policy formulation and implementation. It also requires strengthening the capacity of RUWCs to plan and decide on water resources.

Women's participation in community affairs, which is often driven by discriminatory social and cultural norms, are still challenges to be overcome. RUWCs are still heavily male-dominated. Women's participation in water resource management is still limited. Gender equality in water resource management is important for sustainable development. It will require strengthening water governance by addressing its social and gender dimensions. This requires strengthening women's participation in decision-making processes, including policy formulation and implementation. It also requires strengthening the capacity of RUWCs to plan and decide on water resources.

### Priority 2 - Promoting Collective Farming among Smallholders

Collective farming is a viable option for smallholder farmers to achieve economies of scale. Businesses will together in the long run – through likely bonded relationships, a mutual economic benefit. When farmers change to collective farming, they can reduce their costs and increase their income. Individual cultivation are subject to terms of labor, technology adoption. Collective farming and contract farming provide smallholders with better farm technologies or a bulk input business. It is important to promote the development of collective farming among smallholder farmers.

# Opportunities for developing women-led agri-enterprises should also be explored, particularly in vegetable production

## Priority 5 - Targeting Youth Farmers as Change Agents

Young people under the age of 30 represent 2/3 of the current population and continue to grow at around 5% per year. The youth are increasingly becoming a major force for change in agriculture and rural areas. Youth are not often officially recognized but between rural and urban is the place where young people can be found. Young people aged between 18 and 24 years old are particularly important as they are entering their most productive period of life. They are also at an age when they are most open to new ideas, technologies and approaches to farming and agribusiness. They are more capable physically and their can span many effective years in the field. Youth can be a major force for change in agriculture if they are targeted as change agents in the rural change process within their communities and thus create a demand-driven market for agricultural inputs and services. This is particularly true of young women who have been identified as between 80-95% of those who are actively engaged in farming. As farmers, they can contribute to food security and nutrition, income generation, employment and business development.

### 4.4 Anticipated Trends during Project Life and Beyond

The trends would become other opportunities for change agents and youth groups as well as the communities where agricultural projects are implemented and put back into operation:

- Increased labor market is anticipated as most increasing demand of intensive farm production systems will require more labor.
- Labor (including data women such as crop care, harvesting) will be in high demand.
- Production systems that include labor-saving technologies will be increasingly adopted to reduce the need for labor.
- Opportunities to target market segments by bringing farmers premium products to various and additional markets that bring higher prices.
- Increased income from study or work that leads to increased number of crops per year and increased crop yields through better crop production based on improved variety and crop management.
- Increased demand for agricultural services such as input supply, credit, marketing, and extension services.
- Particularly given women's roles in food processing, there will be an increasing demand for value-added products.
- Reduced labor exchange because of (1) future mechanization irrigation and (2) increased labor market demand.
- Reduced labor exchange because of (1) future mechanization irrigation and (2) increased labor market demand.

# GENDER ACTION & SOCIAL INCLUSION PLAN

## 5.1 Approach to Developing Gender Action & Social Inclusion Plan (GASIP)

The GASIP is aligned with the national framework of the project. The aim is to ensure the actions proposed in the plan are consistent with the objectives and principles outlined in the national framework. This includes ensuring that gender issues are addressed in activities, GASIP are promoted based on empirical evidence gathered from the project area, and that the plan is developed through a participatory approach. The plan will be reviewed annually to ensure it remains relevant and effective. It will also provide an opportunity for continuous monitoring of progress towards the objectives. This plan will be implemented over the life of the project.

## 5.2 Mainstreaming GASIP into Project Implementation Plan

The following plan is a guide to projects to maximize benefits, youth and social inclusion in all projects.

With regard to gender, the aim is to strengthen women's leadership capacity as well as to contribute to household and community resilience. In line with the five SDGs of IAAO Gender Policy, this will be done by:

1. **Advocating gender workshops.** This will be done by raising awareness on the importance of gender equality in all aspects of project implementation. It will also include training on gender-responsive planning, farming schedules, irrigation systems, financial management, business (that are essential for them to succeed).

2. **Promoting women's economic empowerment.** This will be done by enhancing women's access to skills and business development training, as well as credit and input target market chains. It will also include promoting the use of gender-responsive technologies that can help women participate in project training. This will help them address the potential impacts of climate change on their livelihoods. It will also promote the use of labor-saving technologies (such as water pumps) to reduce the time spent on farming and other household chores, allowing more time for income-generating activities.

3. **Strengthening women's role in decision-making both at the household and community level.** It is important to ensure that women have a voice in decisions that affect them. This will be done by promoting gender equality in all project areas, as well as encouraging joint planning and implementation of projects with men. It will also promote the participation of women in local governance structures.

Key activities for support for implementation of the project's GASIP include training of women staff and implementing actions on gender and social inclusion, including the use of tools and approaches to promote gender equality and social inclusion.

## 1.4 Community Governance

### Gender Mainstreaming GAP

- Women's PHU is carrying out consultation/training/workshop with effective participation of female representatives of target households and female headed households.
- Women are encouraged to participate in all aspects of planning, training, and workshops.
- Women are encouraged to participate in all aspects of planning, training, and workshops.

## 1.5 Project Target Groups

### Women's PHU

- Women should be made to avoid actions that participate in previous activities, including consultation and planning meetings, training, and workshops.
- The women will be empowered to participate in planning and decision-making activities to support households' income and food security.
- Gender transformative approaches such as GAS should be used to promote joint decision-making at the household level.
- Women's PHU is carrying out consultation/training/workshop with effective participation of female representatives of target households and female headed households.

## 4.2 Monitoring and Evaluation Arrangement

### Gender Mainstreaming GAP

- The project will be monitored annually for a 3-year assessment, with indicator performance indicators (See Annex 1 - Section A Summary of GAP Performance Indicators). Execution of the GAP will be considered successful if the following criteria are met:
  - The project is implemented in accordance with the approved plan.
  - The project is implemented effectively and efficiently.
  - The project is implemented on time.

# Annex 1 - Template of Updating Annual GASP

- Prepared by: PMU of ______________ province
- Date:
- Fiscal Year:

## How to Use the Template

- The template has two parts, including Part A (GASP Initiations) and Part B (Annual GASP).
- You need to fill in both parts for each fiscal year.
- You can report budget allocation, especially for activities listed in Project's GASP for the next fiscal year. This should be done before the beginning of each fiscal year such that targets tentatively set for mid-year and end-year are achieved.

## Please Note

### For Part A
- The percentage of IP households receiving project benefits are calculated based on the List of Projects.
- Regularly update the list of new projects and remove those that have been completed or no longer exist in the area of operation.
- Activities proposed in the Annual GASP should be aligned with respective Component and Output (based on project's Logical Framework) to allow annual monitoring, and evaluate income by month.
- Each proposed activity should come with a proposed budget, indicating the period (in months) from start to end. The budget should include costs for human resources, materials, and other expenses required for the planned result of the activity, and relevant stakeholders who need to participate to realize the activity.

# Summary of feedback from consulted participants for gender assessment

## Comments
- Participants are concerned that the presence of workers which could increase the risk of sexual abuse of women and children along the project site.


---
**Page 224**
---

- Drug trafficking and consuming among workers may cause unsafe environment for both workers and employers.
- The contamination caused may generate bad and dangerous road conditions which may lead to fatality.
- Waste generated from worker camps and construction activities during the project could contaminate water sources in the area.
- Unique cultural distribution among foreigners participating as domestic helpers, office-based employees, and sex workers may cause social problems.
- Massive labor force deciding not to relocate animal stocks and other cattle portions are prompting farmers to sell their animals at a cheaper price.

### Suggestions from the consulted people
- Relevant government departments should provide training on gender issues, violence prevention, labor laws, and human rights.
- The project should conduct gender education in the districts to prevent and address gender-based violence.
- Drug trafficking is a workplace issue that needs to be addressed by the relevant authorities.
- The distribution equality regarding water allocation from the system.
- The government should provide training on gender issues for farmers, workers, and drivers.
- The government should also provide training on gender issues for farmers, workers, and drivers.
- The contamination causes must collaborate with local authorities in all activities to make a proper investigation.
- The government, community leaders, and relevant authorities should implement proper signage and water the roads, as well as schedule regular cleaning of the area.
- The government should also provide training on gender issues for farmers, workers, and drivers.

The next greater social audit feedback from stakeholder consultation provided at Annex 1 of stand alone Stakeholder Engagement Plan.
